 Obtaining the Spectral Function of Moiré Graphene Heavy-Fermions Using Iterative
                                                      Perturbation Theory

          Dumitru Călugăru,1,2,∗ Haoyu Hu,3,1,∗ Lorenzo Crippa,4,5 Gautam Rai,6,7 Nicolas Regnault,8,1,9
               Tim O. Wehling,6,7 Roser Valentí,10 Giorgio Sangiovanni,4 and B. Andrei Bernevig1,3,11,†
                       1Department of Physics, Princeton University, Princeton, New Jersey 08544, USA
           2Rudolf Peierls Centre for Theoretical Physics, University of Oxford, Oxford OX1 3PU, United Kingdom
          3Donostia International Physics Center, P. Manuel de Lardizabal 4, 20018 Donostia-San Sebastián, Spain
            4Institut für Theoretische Physik und Astrophysik and Würzburg-Dresden Cluster of Excellence ct.qmat,
                                          Universität Würzburg, 97074 Würzburg, Germany
            5I. Institute of Theoretical Physics, Universität Hamburg, Notkestraße 9-11, 22607 Hamburg, Germany
           6I. Institute of Theoretical Physics, University of Hamburg, Notkestraße 9-11, 22607 Hamburg, Germany
                7The Hamburg Centre for Ultrafast Imaging, Luruper Chaussee 149, 22761 Hamburg, Germany
        8Center for Computational Quantum Physics, Flatiron Institute, 162 5th Avenue, New York, NY 10010, USA
                                       9Laboratoire de Physique de l’Ecole normale supérieure,
                                         ENS, Université PSL, CNRS, Sorbonne Université,
                                Université Paris-Diderot, Sorbonne Paris Cité, 75005 Paris, France

                                   Max-von-Laue-Strasse 1, 60438 Frankfurt am Main, Germany
                                  11IKERBASQUE, Basque Foundation for Science, Bilbao, Spain

                  The spectral functions of twisted bilayer graphene (TBG) in the absence of strain have re-
               cently been investigated in both the symmetric [1–4] and symmetry-broken [4] phases using dy-
               namical mean-field theory (DMFT). The theoretically predicted Mott-Hubbard bands and gapless
               semimetallic state at half-filling have since been confirmed experimentally. Here, we develop several
               second-order perturbation theory approaches to the topological heavy-fermion (THF) model of TBG
               and twisted symmetric trilayer graphene (TSTG). In the symmetric phase, we adapt, implement,
               and benchmark an iterative perturbation theory (IPT) impurity solver within DMFT, enabling
               computationally efficient yet accurate spectral function calculations. We present momentum- and
               energy-resolved spectra over a broad range of temperatures and fillings for both symmetric and
               symmetry-broken states. In addition, we derive analytic expressions for the spectral function within
               the “Hubbard-I” approximation of the THF model and, as expected, find that while it provides a
               tractable description of Mott physics, it does not capture the low-energy Kondo peak or the finite
               lifetime broadening of the bands. Our methodology can be extended to include strain, lattice relax-
               ation, and parameter variations, thereby allowing systematic predictions of TBG and TSTG spectral
               properties across a wide range of physical regimes. Because our perturbative approaches are far less
               computationally intensive than DMFT with numerically exact impurity solvers, they can be used
               to efficiently benchmark and scan extensive phase diagrams of the THF parameters, paving the way
               for full DMFT analyses of the TBG spectral function in the presence of strain and relaxation [5].

FIG. 1. Spectral functions of TBG within the THF model in its symmetry-broken phases. Both the DOS and the momentum-
resolved spectral functions are shown, as defined in Eqs. (9) and (10). Each column corresponds to a specific symmetry-broken
phase or to the band insulator, as indicated above the panels. Panels (a)-(d) illustrate the|ν = 4⟩ band-insulating state: (a)
shows the doping- and energy-dependence of the DOS over a range|∆ν|≤ 0.5 around integer filling, while (b)-(d) display the
k-resolved spectral function at integer filling, electron doping, and hole doping, respectively, with the filling indicated inside
each panel. The same arrangement is used for the other correlated phases. The simulation temperature is indicated above each
panel. The color scale is described in the main text.

      Appendix I: Review of the topological heavy fermion models for twisted graphene heterostructures

  This appendix succinctly summarizes the topological heavy fermion (THF) models for twisted bilayer graphene
(TBG) [186] and twisted symmetric trilayer graphene (TSTG) [194]. We first discuss the single-particle THF Hamil-
tonians for the two twisted graphene system. We then give a brief overview of the interaction THF Hamiltonian.
Additionally, we specify the single-particle and interaction THF parameters utilized in this work, which are identical
with those adopted in Ref. [254].



                                               1.    The single-particle THF model

                                              a.  The single-particle THF model of TBG

  TBG comprises two stacked graphene layers rotated at an angle θ relative to one another [6]. The single-particle
THF model Hamiltonian for TBG is given by [186]
                                                                           X                                       !
                    HTBG0      = X    X      aa′  (k) ˆc†k,a,η,sˆck,a,η,s +     hcf,ηaα   (k) ˆc†k,a,η,s ˆfk,α,η,s +h.c.

                               |k|≤Λcη,sa,a′hcc,η                            a,α

                            + X       hff,ηαα′  (k) ˆf†k,α,η,s ˆfk,α′,η,s,                                                (S1.1)
                               k,α,α′η,s

where “+h.c.” denotes the addition of the Hermitian conjugate.  The THF model for TBG features two types of
fermions denoted by thef- andc-electron operators in Eq. (S1.1). Thef-fermions are localized at the moiré AA-sites
and transform as a pair of px±ipy orbitals. The operator  ˆf†k,α,η,s creates an f-fermion of spin s  =↑,↓ at moiré
momentum k in valley η =±, having an orbital index α = 1, 2, with α = 1 (α = 2) corresponding to the f-electron
state transforming as a px +ipy (px−ipy) orbital. The real-space f-electron operators are defined according to
                                                  ˆf†          √     X    ˆf†
                                                   R,α,η,s =    1N0        k,α,η,se−ik·R,                               (S1.2)
                                                                      k

whereN0 is the total number of moiré unit cells, which are labeled by their position lattice vector R. Thec-fermions
form semimetallic, highly dispersive, and anomalous electronic bands. At a given k point, the c-electron states are
labeled by their spin s =↑,↓, valley η =±, and orbital numbers 1≤ a≤ 4. At the ΓM point (k = 0) of the moiré
Brillouin zone (BZ) shown in Fig. S6, the ˆc†k,a,η,s form a Γ3 (Γ1⊕Γ2) irreducible (reducible) representation fora = 1, 2
(a = 3, 4).
  The matrix blocks appearing in Eq. (S1.1) are given by [186]
                                                                0       v⋆ (ηkxσ0 +ikyσz)
                                      hcc,η (k) =    v∗ (ηkxσ0−ikyσz)     Mσz                         ,                      (S1.3)
                                                    γσ0 +v′∗ (ηkxσx +kyσy)
                                      hcf,η (k) =        v′′∗ (ηkxσx−kyσy)           e−|k|2λ22  ,                            (S1.4)

                                     hff,η (k) =O (t)≈ 0,                                                   (S1.5)

with 0 denoting the zero matrix. Note that a cutoff Λc exists for the momenta of thec-electrons, andσi (i = 0,x,y,z)
denoting the Pauli matrices acting in orbital space. The choice of Λc will be discussed in Appendix [II1]. Throughout
this work and Ref. [254], we consider the following numerical values for the single-particle parameters appearing in
Eq. (S1.1): λ  =  0.3375|aM1|, γ  =−24.75 meV, M  =  3.697 meV, v⋆  =−4.303 eVÅ, v′⋆  =  1.623 eVÅ, and v′′⋆  =
−0.0332 eVÅ. These THF parameters correspond to θ = 1.05◦, w1 = 110 meV, vF  = 5.944 eVÅ,|K+| = 1.703Å−1,
andw0/w1 = 0.8 [186], whereθ denotes the TBG twist angle,vF and K+ are the single layer graphene Fermi velocity
and wave vector of the K point, whilew0 andw1 are, respectively, the interlayer tunneling amplitudes at the AA and
AB sites. Near the magic angle, which will be the focus of this work, the f-electrons are dispersionless, which implies
that their nearest neighbor hopping amplitude t vanishes. As a result, we will approximate the matrix block from
Eq. (S1.5) to zero.

                                  FIG. S6. Moiré Brillouin zone (BZ) of the THF model. The first BZ denoted by the blue hexagon is centered at the ΓM point.
                                  We also explicitly label the KM, K′M and MM high-symmetry points. Additionally, the three vectors qi (for 1≤ i≤ 3) are
                                  indicated by the black arrows.


                                                                                b.  The single-particle THF model of TSTG

                                     TSTG consists of three graphene layers: the top and bottom layers are located directly above one another and are
                                  rotated by an angle θ relative to the middle layer [93, 95, 100, 111]. The THF model of TSTG can be obtained form
                                  the TBG one in Eq. (S1.1) by the addition of a high-velocity Dirac (d) fermion to the latter [194]
                                                                                       HTSTG0        =HTBG0      +HD0  +HE0.                                 (S1.6)
                                  The second and third terms of Eq. (S1.6) are given by [194]

                                                 HD0  = X        hdd,ηαα′  (p) ˆd†p,α,η,s ˆdp,α′,η,s  and  HE0  = X   hdf,ηαα′ (p) ˆd†p,α′,η,s ˆfp+ηq1,α,η,s +h.c.,      (S1.7)
                                                          p≤Λd                                                  p≤Λdα,η,s
                                                         α,α′,η,s
                                  and correspond, respectively, to the Dirac Hamiltonian of the d-fermions and the f-d single-particle hybridization.
                                  The operator  ˆd†p,α,η,s creates a high-velocity Dirac fermion at of momentum p and spin s  =↑,↓, in valley η  =±,
                                  belonging to the single-layer graphene sublattice α = 1, 2. Note that the momenta of the d-fermions are measured
                                  from ηq1, which, as depicted in Fig. S6, corresponds to the  K′M (KM) point in valley η  =  + (η  =−).  At the
                                  single-particle level, the d- and c-electrons are uncoupled, whereas the d- and f-fermions only couple in the presence
                                  of a non-zero perpendicular displacement fieldE. The matrix blocks of the last two terms in Eq. (S1.6) are
                                                                                   hdd,η (p) =vF (ηpxσx +pyσy),                                   (S1.8)
                                                                                   hdf,η (p) =M1E (σ0−iησz)e−|p|2λ22  ,                              (S1.9)

                                  where vF is the graphene Fermi velocity, while M1 is the d-f coupling constant.  Similarly, to the c-electrons in
                                  Eq. (S1.1), a momentum cutoff Λd was introduced for thed-electrons’ momenta, which will be fixed in Appendix [II1].
                                     The first term of Eq. (S1.6), HTBG0    assumes the same form as in Eq. (S1.6), but the values of the γ, v′′∗, and M
                                  parameters now depend on the displacement fieldE according to [194]
                                                                       γ→γ +BγE2,  v′′∗→v′′∗ +Bv′′∗E2,  M→M +BME2.                    (S1.10)
                                  The numerical values of the parameters that we employ for TSTG are consistent with those of Refs. [194, 254] and
                                  correspond to a twist angle of θ = 1.4703◦ (with the same values of the interlayer tunneling amplitudes, graphene
                                  Fermi velocity and K-point wave vector as in the case of TBG from Appendix [I1a]).  Specifically, we employ
                                  λ  =  0.3359|aM1|, γ  = −33.16 meV, M  =  7.01 meV, v⋆  = −4.301 eVÅ, v′⋆  =  1.625 eVÅ, v′′⋆  = −0.0346 eVÅ,
                                  M1 =−0.1394, Bγ =−3.75× 10−4 meV−1, Bv′′∗  = 7.65× 103 eV−1Å, and BM = 3.28× 10−4 meV−1.

In Eq. (S1.19)Uξ =  e24πϵ0ϵξ is the interaction energy scale (in SI units), with ϵ being the dielectric constant and ξ, the

where|G0⟩ denotes a state at the charge neutrality point. We take this state to be such that

                                     ⟨G0|ˆc†k,a,η,sˆck′,a′,η′,s′|G0⟩ = 12δk,k′δaa′δηη′δss′,

                                    ⟨G0| ˆf†k,α,η,s ˆfk′,α′,η′,s′|G0⟩ = 12δk,k′δαα′δηη′δss′,

Additionally, in Eq. (S1.13),⟨R, R′⟩ denotes nearest-neighbor lattice sites. For the twist angle and tunneling ratio
chosen in Appendix [I1a], as well as the relative permittivity and screening length chosen below Eq. (S1.19), the six
interaction parameters (W1 =W2 and W3 =W4 follows by symmetry [186]) are given by [186]

                  U1 = 57.95 meV,          U2 = 2.239 meV,          W1 =W2 = 44.03 meV,
                    J = 16.38 meV,          K = 4.887 meV,          W3 =W4 = 50.2 meV,        (S1.21)

while the c-c interaction potential is the same as in Eq. (S1.19).

                                            b.  The interaction THF Hamiltonian for TSTG

  The mirror-even and mirror-odd fermions of TSTG are always coupled through density-density interactions, even
in the absence of a perpendicularly applied displacement field [111]. As a result the TSTG interaction Hamiltonian
of TSTG (HTSTGI    ) can be constructed from the one of TBG by simply adding three terms to the latter,

                                                 HTSTGI        =HTBGI      +HdV  +HcdV  +HfdW,                             (S1.22)

which correspond to the Coulomb repulsion terms between thed-electrons (HdV), thec- andd-electrons (HcdV ), as well
as the f- and d-electrons (HfdW ) [194]. These three additional terms are given respectively by [194]
        HdV  =      1         X               X             X     V (q) : ˆd†

                         |p1|,|p1|≤Λd          q           α,η,s
                                       |p1+q|,|p2+q|≤Λd   α′,η′,s′
                          X         X         X
       HcdV   =     1Ω0N0                           V (q) :ˆc†k1+q,a,η,sˆck1,a,η,s:: ˆd†p2−q,α′,η′,s′ ˆdp2,α′,η′,s′:,               (S1.24)
                        |k1|≤Λc       q       a,η,s
                        |p2|<Λd  |k1+q|≤Λc  α′,η′,s′

                       k1|p2|≤Λd  q       α,η,s

where we note that the state|G0⟩ at charge neutrality relative to which the normal ordering is defined obeys

                                   ⟨G0| ˆd†p,α,η,s ˆdp′,α′,η′,s′|G0⟩ = 12δp,p′δαα′δηη′δss′,

Finally, forthesingle-particleparameterschoseninAppendix[I1b]andtheCoulombrepulsionassumedinEq.(S1.19),
the THF interaction parameters for TSTG are given by [194]

        U1 = 91.5 meV,     U2 = 6.203 meV,     W1 =W2 = 88.54 meV,
         J = 24.25 meV,     K = 7.061 meV,     W3 =W4 = 97.67 meV,     Wfd = 94.71 meV.      (S1.27)

As explained in Section IIIC3 of Ref. [254], the approximate increase by a factor of√2 in the interaction parameters
of TSTG relative to the ones of TBG from Eq. (S1.21) can be traced back to the magic twist angle of TSTG, which
is itself larger by a factor of√2 than the one of TBG [194].


                                    Appendix II: Hartree-Fock theory for the THF model

  In this appendix, we give a brief overview of the Hartree-Fock theory for the THF model, with the main goal of
formalizing the notation that will be used in subsequent sections. The reader is referred to Refs. [186, 194] for a more
in-depth treatment of the subject. We start by introducing a useful notation that treats the f-,c-, and (in the case of
TSTG)d-electrons on equal footing and define the Green’s function for the system. Next, we derive the Hartree-Fock
Hamiltonian of the system assuming a generic ground state.  In addition to the zero-temperature regime already
considered in Refs. [186, 194], we also consider the finite temperature case. Finally, we list the symmetry-breaking
correlated ground state candidates that we consider in this work and in Ref. [254] and derive the implications of the
many-body charge-conjugation symmetry on the THF model spectral function.


                                                          1.    Generic notation

  The c-fermions (for both TBG and TSTG) and d-fermions (for TSTG) are only defined within a limited region
around the  ΓM and  KM (or  K′M, depending on the valley) points, respectively.  In order to restore the system’s
symmetries, Refs. [186, 194] set the momentum cutoffs, Λc and Λd, to infinity (or to a sufficiently large value in the
                                                                                                                                                                    29

                      numerical implementation). In our numerical approach, we will extend these two cutoffs so that the ˆc†k,a,η,s ( ˆd†p,α,η,s)
                      fermion covers exactly one BZ around the ΓM (ηq1) point.
                        We introduce a notation that treats all the fermionic species on equal footing. As such, for TBG, we define the
                      ˆγ†k,η,i,s fermions (for 1≤i≤ 6)
                                                                                (
                                                                     ˆγ†           ˆc†k,η,i,s,      for    1≤i≤ 4
                                                                      k,η,i,s≡     ˆf†
                                                                                    k,η,i−4,s,    for    5≤i≤ 6.                            (S2.1)
                      For TSTG, we introduce two additional fermions

                                                                       ˆγ†k,η,i,s≡  ˆd†Pk,η,i−6,s  for    7≤i≤ 8,                              (S2.2)
                      where here and in what follows we will let Pk denote the image of k−ηq1 in the first BZ. For simplicity, we also
                      define the following single-particle Hamiltonian matrices corresponding respectively to the THF models of TBG and
                      TSTG, whose blocks are given by Eqs. (S1.3) to (S1.5), (S1.8) and (S1.9),

                                                                                                                                               
                                hTBG,η (k) =                                  ,  hTSTG,η (k) =                                                    .        (S2.3)


                      In Eq. (S2.3), the dimensions of the blocks are explicitly indicated outside each matrix. To make the expressions valid
                      even away from the magic angle, we have included the f-electron single-particle Hamiltonian hff,η (k). At the magic
                      angle, hff,η (k)≈ 0.

                                                  2.    The Hartree-Fock Hamiltonian

  In this section we briefly review the Hartree-Fock theory for the THF model. We start from the THF interaction
Hamiltonian, which, for TBG, can be rewritten as

from Eqs. (S1.12) and (S1.13) give rise to the following terms in the interaction tensor

2 In general, symmetry breaking is promoted by the Fock term [186]. For the TBG and TSTG correlated states, the symmetry breaking
  mainly occurs within the f-electron modes. As such, it is reasonable to ingore the Fock channels of the HdV and HcdV  terms, which do

  We note that at self-consistency, the Hartree-Fock Hamiltonian matrix from Eq. (S2.25) or Eq. (S2.26) and the
density matrix defined in Eq. (S2.17) are related by

                                         ϱT (k) =expβ hMF (k)−µ1 + 1	−1− 121,                        (S2.27)

where 1 denotes the identity matrix. Because hMF (k) itself depends on the density matrix ϱ (k), Eq. (S2.27) must
be solved self-consistently, as we will explain in Appendix [II3]. Eq. (S2.27) follows straightforwardly by noting that
within the Hartree-Fock approximation (i.e. with only the Hartree-Fock interaction contribution being included), the
Green’s function of the system is given simply by

                                              G (iωn, k) =(iωn +µ) 1−hMF (k)−1,                            (S2.28)

which implies that the spectral function of the system is a Dirac δ-function,

                                                 A(ω, k) =δ (ω +µ) 1−hMF (k).                              (S2.29)

By substituting Eq. (S2.29) into Eq. (S2.17), one directly recovers Eq. (S2.27). The chemical potential is fixed at each
step by requiring that the filling of the system equals some target value.
  Finally, it is also worth noting that from Eqs. (S2.10), (S2.11) and (S2.28), one can obtain the Hartree-Fock
self-energy of the system (which is first order in the interaction),

                                                         Σ (iωn, k) =hI,MF (k).                                   (S2.30)

The Hartree-Fock self-energy is none other than the Hartree-Fock interaction Hamiltonian.  Being frequency-
independent, the former is often called the static contribution to the self-energy.


                                             3.    Correlated ground state candidates

                                                             a.  Model states

  For both TBG and TSTG, in this work, we consider nine different correlated ground state candidates at different
integer fillings (together with their charge conjugated counterparts). These are summarized in Table S1. Within
Hartree-Fock, the wave function of each state (or equivalently its self-consistent density matrix) is obtained at zero
temperaturebystartingfromtheindicatedparentstates[186],aswillbeexplainedbelow. Theparentstatesthemselves
are obtained by fully-populatingf-fermion bands on top of a half-filled Fermi sea (|FS⟩) of justc-electrons in the case
of TBG or both c- and d-electrons in the case of TSTG. The states we consider here have been shown to be ground
states or low-energy states in TBG [186] or TSTG at low values of the displacement fieldE and in the absence of
strain [194].
  To obtain the self-consistent density matrix for a given type of ground state candidate at filling ν0, we start from an
initialguessofthedensitymatrix(i.e., obtaineddirectlyfromtheparentstatesinTableS1)andformtheHartree-Fock
Hamiltonian according to Eq. (S2.22) or Eq. (S2.26). The latter is then diagonalized as
                                          X


                                         i′,η′,s′hMFiηs;i′η′s′ (k)φn;i′η′s′ (k) =ϵn (k)φn;iηs (k),                       (S2.31)

where ϵn (k) and φn;i′η′s′ (k) denote the n-th Hartree-Fock energy and eigenvector. A new mean-field density matrix
is then constructed according to
                                    ϱiηs;i′η′s′ (k) =X              1

                                                        n   eβ(ϵn(k)−µ) + 1φ∗n;iηs (k)φn;i′η′s′ (k),                    (S2.32)

where the chemical potential is fixed by requiring that the total filling of the system equals the initial filling, i.e.
ν =ν0. With the new density matrix obtained, the Hartree-Fock Hamiltonian is then recomputed. The two steps in
Eqs. (S2.31) and (S2.32) are repeated until self-consistency is achieved.
  Numerically, we accelerate convergence using the direct inversion in the iterative subspace (DIIS) algorithm [300–
302] at both zero and finite temperature.  Additionally, at zero temperature, we employ the relaxed constrained
algorithm (RCA) [302, 303], as well as the so-called “energy-DIIS” (EDIIS) algorithm [302, 304].  Convergence is

TABLE S1.  Correlated ground state candidates considered in this work.  For each filling ν we list the correlated ground
state candidate, as well as the wave functions of the corresponding parent states [186, 194]. Each correlated state is obtained
using zero-temperature Hartree-Fock starting from the corresponding parent state. In turn, the parent states are obtained by
occupying (ν + 4)f-electron bands on top of a half-filled Fermi sea of c- (for TBG) orc- andd- (for TSTG) electrons (denoted
as|FS⟩). For all integer fillings−4 < ν≤ 0, we consider valley-polarized (VP) states. At ν  =−2, 0, we also consider the
Kramers intervalley-coherent (K-IVC) states [161]. At ν  =−3 there is only one maximal intervalley-coherent (IVC) state,
while forν =−1, the ground state has been shown to have two filled K-IVC f-electron bands and one filled VP one [173, 186].
Finally, we note that for TBG, these correlated states are insulating [186], while for TSTG they are metallic [194]. The ground
state candidates at positive integer fillings are obtained from the ones at negative integer fillings using the charge conjugation
operatorP (for TBG [111, 113, 132, 136, 186]) orP′ (for TSTG [111, 194]), which are defined, respectively, in Eqs. (S2.33)
and (S2.35).


ascertained by noting that at self-consistency, Eq. (S2.27) implies that the Hartree-Fock Hamiltonian computed from
Eqs. (S2.24) and (S2.26) and the transpose of the density matrix computed from Eqs. (S2.31) and (S2.32) commute.
  We note that at a given integer filling ν0, the self-consistent Hartree-Fock density matrices obtained starting
from different parent states will generically be different, as they will respect the symmetries of the corresponding
parent states3. For example, at ν  =−2, the self-consistent state|ν =−2, VP⟩ will be valley polarized, while the
self-consistent one|ν =−2, K− IVC⟩ will be Kramers intervalley-coherent. For both TBG [121, 137–143, 145, 147–
153, 155–170, 173, 177, 181–184, 186, 187, 190, 192] and TSTG [179, 185, 194], a myriad of correlated ground states
have been proposed. Becausethe different states at a given integerfilling are close in energy (with the precise hierarchy
being determined by effects such as strain, relaxation, etc.), we consider multiple correlated ground state candidates.
Nevertheless, the differences in their thermoelectric transport [254] are primarily quantitative rather than qualitative.


                                                      b.  Many-body charge conjugation

  Given that the THF models for both TBG and TSTG without lattice relaxation possess many-body (unitary)
charge conjugation symmetry [111, 113, 132, 136, 186, 194], we can restrict, without loss of generality, to positive
fillings withν≥ 0. For concreteness, the many-body charge conjugation operatorP is defined for TBG as the single-
particle transformationC2zTP (whereP,T, andC2z are the unitary particle-hole, the antiunitary time-reversal, and
two-fold z-directed unitary rotation symmetry operations) followed by an exchange of the creation and annihilation
operators [113, 186],
                                                Pˆγ†k,i,η,sP−1 =X
                                                                    i′,η′ [D (P)]i′η′;iη ˆγ−k,i′,η′,s,                          (S2.33)





3 This is because the self-consistent density matrix is obtained iteratively. The Hartree-Fock Hamiltonian obtained from a density matrix
  obeying some of the symmetries of the system will also obey those symmetries. Conversely, diagonalizing a Hartree-Fock Hamiltonian
  obeying some of the symmetries of the system will lead to a density matrix obeying those symmetries. A more in-depth discussion is
  provided in Ref. [4].
                                                                                                                                           34

with the representation matrix ofP being

                                                     D (P) = [σy⊕σy⊕ (−σy)]τz.                                (S2.34)

In Eq. (S2.34),σµ (µ = 0,x,y,z) represent the Pauli and identity matrices acting on thei = 1, 2,i = 3, 4, andi = 5, 6,
orbital subspaces (and additionally in the i = 7, 8 orbital subspace in the case of TSTG), while τµ (µ = 0,x,y,z)
are the Pauli and identity matrices operating on the valley subspace. For TBG, the many-body THF Hamiltonian
is symmetric under the action ofP (i.e., HTBG,P  =  0). In contrast, TSTG at a finite displacement field does
not maintain this symmetry. Instead, it exhibits a spatial many-body charge conjugation symmetry, represented by
P′ [111]. The latter is defined as the combined antiunitary single-particle transformation mzC2xC2zTP (where C2x
denotes a two-folder rotation around the x axis and mz is the mirror symmetry operator perpendicular to the z
axis) followed by an interchange between the creation and annihilation operators [111]. Its action on the TSTG THF
fermions is described by

                                          P′ˆγ†k,i,η,sP′−1 =X
                                                               i′,η′ [D (P′)]i′η′;iη ˆγ−C2xk,i′,η′,s,                        (S2.35)

where the representation matrix ofP′ reads as

                                              D (P′) = [iσz⊕iσz⊕ (−iσz)⊕ (−σ0)]τz.                           (S2.36)

The spatial many-body charge conjugation operator commutes with the TSTG many-body Hamiltonian both in the
absence or in the presence of a displacement field,HTSTG,P′ = 0.
  The two charge-conjugation operatorsP andP′ map states at filling ν to states at filling−ν. Alternatively, in
the grand canonical ensemble, the charge conjugation operators map phases at chemical potential µ = µ0 to ones
at chemical potential µ =−µ0. Let the Green’s function of the system at chemical potential µ =±µ0 be given by
G±µ0iηs;i′η′s′ (iωn, k), such that

                     Appendix III: Self-energy correction in the THF model beyond Hartree-Fock

  The transport properties of electronic systems are governed not only by the dispersion of the charge-one excitations,
but also by their lifetime [292]. While the former can be readily determined using the Hartree-Fock method (i.e., to
first-orderintheinteraction), assuccinctlydiscussedinAppendix[II],thelattermanifestsas second-order effectsinthe
interaction. This appendix focuses on obtaining the corrections to the electrons’ self-energy within the THF model,
thereby incorporating finite-lifetime effects.  Throughout this work, we will only consider elastic electron-electron
scattering and ignore the effects of disorder.
  For this appendix, we will assume that the system is in an ordered phase, specifically one of the integer-filled corre-
lated ground state candidates described in Table S1, or an ordered phase achieved by doping these states away from
integer fillings. The discussion of the fully-symmetric (unordered) phase will be addressed separately in Appendix [IV].
  We start by outlining the second-order interaction contributions to the self-energy of the system. A basic familiarity
with Feynmann diagrams will be assumed in this appendix [292]. A self-contained alternative derivation that does
not rely on Feynman diagrams, but instead uses the path integral formulation is provided in Appendix [VI3]. Within
a self-consistent treatment, we will demonstrate that only two diagrams contribute at the second order. Because
they are dispersionless and their onsite repulsion is the largest energy scale of the system in TBG [186] (and among
the largest energy-scales in TSTG [194]), we focus solely on the second-order corrections to the f-electrons’ self-
energy, which arise from the interaction of the f-electrons between themselves (i.e., the HU1 and HU2 terms of the
Hamiltonian). We expect that the strongly-dispersing c-electrons (and d-electrons in the case of TSTG) will have a
much longer lifetime. As a result, we only consider a small broadening of their spectral function which is added by
hand. As we explain in Appendix [III4], provided that this broadening factor is smaller than any other energy scale
of the problem, the exact value is not important.
  Following Refs. [265–267], we will then assume that the second-order self-energy correction for the f-electrons is
site-diagonal (to be defined rigorously in Appendix [III3]), or, alternatively, momentum-independent. This will enable
us to self-consistently compute the second-order correction to the self-energy of the f-electrons. After deriving the
formula of the second-order self-energy correction, we will also give a brief overview of our numerical implementation.
                                                                                                                                                                                          36






























                                         FIG. S7.  Feynman diagrams for the THF model of TBG. The non-interacting Matsubara Green’s function (i.e., the bare
                                         propagator)oftheTHFmodelforTBGisrepresentedschematicallyin(a)byaline. Theprocesscorrespondstothepropagation
                                         of a  ˆγ†k,i′,η′,s′ fermion into a  ˆγ†k,i,η,s one as indicated by the arrow on the line (which therefore indicates the “flow” of  U (1)
                                         charge).  Additionally, the momentum and imaginary frequency are indicated above the propagator.  The THF interaction
                                         Hamiltonian for TBG can be written formally as in Eq. (S2.20). The corresponding four-fermion interaction vertex is shown in
                                         (b). It corresponds to the scattering of two fermions, ˆγ†k2,l,η4,s4 and ˆγ†k1,j,η2,s2, into the ˆγ†k2−q,k,η3,s3 and ˆγ†k1+q,i,η1,s1 fermions.
                                         The amplitude of the scattering process is indicated below the diagram.  Note that even though the interaction happens
                                         instantaneously, conventionally it is represented by a “photon” line [292], as explained in text. Finally, (c) shows the infinite
                                         summation of diagrams equivalent to Dyson’s equation. The hatched blob represents the sum of all the 1-particle irreducible
                                         diagrams (i.e., the self-energy diagrams).


                                                                                                     1.    General remarks

                                            We begin by considering the THF interaction Hamiltonian for TBG on general grounds (the discussion for TSTG
                                         follows analogously).  The (matrix) fermion propagator of the THF model is shown schematically by a line, as
                                         depicted in Fig. S7(a), where the flow of U (1) charge is indicated by an arrow. At the same time, the four-fermion
                                         TBG interaction Hamiltonian in the THF model can be represented by the four-fermion vertex from Fig. S7(a), whose
                                         amplitude can be related to the rank-four tensor from Eq. (S2.20). Even though the interaction is assumed to happen
                                         instantaneously, the four-fermion vertex is still represented by a “photon” line connecting the end of four fermion
                                         propagators. This is a matter of convention [292], as a point-like representation of this scattering process would be
                                         ambiguous and would need to be supplemented by indices (causing unnecessary clutter).

                              FIG. S8. Diagrams that contribute to the self-energy of the THF model up to second order in the interactions. We consider a
                              general four-fermion interaction (that potentially involves all the fermionic species) and whose four-fermion vertex is shown in
                              (a). As explained in the text, it is conventional to represent the interaction with a “photon” line [292], even if the interaction
                              is assumed to happen instantaneously. The remaining diagrams (b)-(i) denote the diagramatic contributions to the electron
                              self-energy. The insertion points are shown as slashed (amputated) fermion propagators. The last two diagrams on the first
                              row are the first-order corrections to the electron self-energy, with (b) denoting the Hartree contribution and (c) denoting the
                              Fock one. The second row shows the second-order contributions to the electron self-energy. It is worth noting that within
                              a self-consistent perturbation scheme, wherein all the internal edges of the diagrams are replaced by dressed, as opposed to
                              bare electron propagators, the second-order diagrams from (d)-(g) are already included in the Hartree-Fock self-consistent
                              self-energy. For instance, the diagram in (d) can be interpreted as replacing the propagators forming a loop in (b) with a
                              propagator dressed by Hartree contributions. As a result, in a self-consistent second-order self-energy scheme, one only needs
                              to consider the diagrams in (h) and (i) at the second-order level.


                              This diagram corresponds to the second term in Eq. (S3.1), namelyG0 (iωn, k) Σ (iωn, k)G0 (iωn, k). The two bare
                              propagators at the ends of the diagram are not part of the self-energy contribution.  Formally, we say that the
                              self-energy diagram (shown schematically as the hatched blob) is amputated, meaning that it has no external lines.
                              However, by attaching two bare propagators at the end of a self-energy diagram, one obtains a bona fide Feynman
                              diagram describing a propagating fermion.  As a result, we say that the self-energy diagrams can be inserted in
                              a propagator, effectively replacingG0 (iωn, k) byG0 (iωn, k) Σ (iωn, k)G0 (iωn, k).  Finally, we mention that when
                              representing the self-energy Feynman diagrams, it is conventional to depict the ends of the diagram to which bare
                              electron propagators can be added to obtain an electron propagation diagram (known also as the insertion points of
                              the self-energy diagram) by slashed fermion propagators.
                                 We want to determine the Feynman diagrams that contribute to the electron self-energy up to and including second
                              order in the interaction. For a generic four-fermion interaction shown in Fig. S8(a), the only such diagrams are shown
                              in Figs. S8(b) to S8(i) [305].
                                 The two diagrams in Figs. S8(b) and S8(c) are, respectively, the Hartree and Fock contributions to the self-energy
                              (which were derived by elementary means in Appendix [II2]) and together form the first-order self-energy correction.
                              The second row of Fig. S8 shows the six diagrams that contribute to the second order in the interaction to the fermion
                              self-energy. We note, however, that, within a self-consistent calculation, only the last two diagrams from Figs. S8(h)
                              and S8(i) need to be considered, as the other four are already included in the Hartree and Fock contributions, as we
                              explain below.
                                 To see why this is so, we note that the self-energy diagrams “dress” the fermion propagators by inserting themselves
                              recursively to infinite order. In a self-consistent calculation, all the propagators used in computing the diagrams are
                              dressed. As such, one can readily see that the diagram in Fig. S8(d) is really only the Hartree self-energy correction
                              fromFig.S8(b)wherethefermionicpropagatorintheloopwasdressedbyoneHartreeself-energycorrection. Similarly,
       h)d)g)e)f)             the diagram in Fig. S8(e) is the Hartree contribution with the propagator dressed by a Fock correction, the one in
                              Fig. S8(f) is the Fock contribution with the propagator dressed by another Fock correction, while the one in Fig. S8(g)
i)     b)a)c)                 is the Fock contribution where the propagator was dressed by another Hartree correction. Since our calculation is self-
                                                                                                                                              38

consistent (and thus employs dressed propagators), only the diagrams in Figs. S8(h) and S8(i) need to be considered
at second order.


                                        b.  Leading contributions to the second-order self-energy

  The main goal of this appendix is to compute the lifetime of the fermions of the THF model from perturbation
theory. The finite lifetime of quasiparticles is linked to the imaginary part of the self-energy correction, which is only
non-vanishing beyond first order in perturbation theory [292]. However, whereas the Hartree and Fock self-energies
can be determined by means of a simple tensor contraction, as shown in Eqs. (S2.24) and (S2.25), and are functions of
momentum only, the second-order self-energy correction is generically a function of both momentum and Matsubara
frequency, and its computation is much more involved. As we will show explicitly in Appendix [III3], the second-order
self-energy also depends on the Green’s function of the systems as whole, rather than just on the density matrix [266].
  Just like the Green’s function, in general, the dynamical self-energy depends on both momentum and Matsubara
frequency. Moreover, as we will show explicitly in Eqs. (S3.9) and (S3.10), the second-order self-energy involves double
summations over momenta (or, equivalently, lattice sites) on top of double summations over Matsubara frequencies.
As such, in order to make our self-consistent second-order perturbation scheme numerically tractable, a number of
approximations are in order:
    • We first note that thef-electrons are dispersionless and have a large onsite repulsion term (which is the largest
       – in TBG – or among the largest – in TSTG – energy scales of the system). Consequently, their scattering rate
       is expected to be significantly higher than that of the strongly dispersing c- and d-electrons, leading to a much
       shorter lifetime. A good approximation is to account for second-order self-energy effects only for thef-electrons.
       For the other fermionic species, we introduce a small broadening factor in their spectral function, effectively
       assigning them a finite but large lifetime. As long as this artificial broadening remains much smaller than that
       of the f-electrons, its precise value is unimportant.
    • When computing the second-order self-energy correction to the f-electrons using the Feynman diagrams in
       Figs. S8(h) and S8(i), all interaction terms in Eqs. (S1.11) and (S1.22), except for HV, HcdV , and HdV (which
       do not involve f-electrons), could, in principle, contribute.  However, interaction terms that couple both f-
       and c- (or f- and d-) electrons, such as HW, would also generate second-order self-energy corrections for the
       c- (or d-) fermions through the same type of diagrams as in Figs. S8(d) to S8(i). To maintain consistency in
       neglecting second-order self-energy corrections for the c- and d-electrons, we must also discard contributions to
       the f-electron self-energy arising from HW, HJ, H ˜J, HK, and HfdW . In other words, performing a second-order
       perturbation expansion in, for instance,W1,3 would require computing and incorporating self-energy corrections
       to the c-electron propagator, which we omit in our calculations.
       As a result, we only consider the second-order self-energy corrections from HU1 and HU24, with the dominant
       contribution arising from HU1.
    • Focusing on f-electrons, we then make another mean-field-type of approximation and take the corresponding
       second-order self-energy correction to be site-diagonal in real space, or otherwise stated,  k-independent in
       momentum space [266]. Such an approximation is at the heart of dynamical mean-field theory methods [3, 4,
       263, 293], and is exact in an infinite number of spatial dimensions [266]. In the same spirit we will also neglect
       correlations between f-electrons from different lattice sites, which are expected to be small due their localized
       nature, as explained around Eq. (S3.11). We note that in the atomic limit of the problem, which will be defined
       and solved analytically in Appendix [V1], both thef-electron Green’s function and self-energy are site-diagonal.
In summary, we will take the second-order contribution to the self-energy to be k-independent and compute it from
HU1 and HU2 using the Feynman diagrams from Figs. S8(h) and S8(i) and ignoring correlation between f-electrons
not residing on the same lattice site.


                                                            2.    Feynman rules

f-electron second-order self-energy correction. As thef-electron interaction is local in real-space we will find it useful

In this form, it is easy to see that each of HU1 and HU2 is a sum between a purely interacting term, a single-particle
contribution and a constant6. It is worth noting that in our self-consistent Hartree-Fock treatment, the single-particle
and constant contributions in Eqs. (S3.6) and (S3.7) are already treated exactly.  Therefore, for the second-order
correction to the self-energy one should only consider the purely interacting terms, i.e. the first terms of each of
Eqs. (S3.6) and (S3.7).
  The bare f-electron propagator line and the f-electron interaction vertex are shown in Fig. S9.  Although the
Coulomb interaction between thef-electrons is assumed to happen instantaneously, we still find it useful to draw the
interaction vertex with a “photon” line. The Feynman rules for constructing and computing the f-electron self-energy
contribution  Σαηs;α′η′s′ (iωn, R− R′) can be summarized below (they were adapted from Ref. [292] to which the
reader is pointed for their complete derivation):
   1.Draw the Feynman diagram for the  f-electron self-energy with all the corresponding vertices and propagators.
       The Feynman diagrams are obtained by connecting together interaction vertices with propagator edges. The
       self-energy diagram should be 1-particle-irreducible and should contain exactly two amputated propagator edges
       labeled as “R,α,η,s” (on the amputated edge for which the charge “leaves” the diagram) and “R′,α′,η′,s′”
       (on the amputated edge for which the charge “enters” the diagram). The amputated edges should also carry
       imaginary frequency iωn is the same direction as the charge.
   2.Label every end point of the interaction “photon” line by a quadruplet of lattice site, orbital type, valley, and
       spin. The “photon” end points connected to the amputated edges should be labeled with the same quadruplet
       as the corresponding edge, as shown in Fig. S9(a).
   3.Assign an imaginary frequency to each internal electron propagator together with an arrow indicating the “flow”
       thereof. The Matsubara frequency should be conserved at each interaction vertex, as shown in Fig. S9(a).
   4.Write down the amplitude corresponding to the diagram in the following way:
        (a)For each    internal propagator line of imaginary frequency iωx where the flow of charge points from
             “R2,α2,η2,s2” to “R1,α1,η1,s1”, associate a factorGf,0α1η1s1;α2η2s2 (iωx, R1− R2).
        (b)For  each  interaction  vertex  where  the  “photon”  line  connects  two   f-states  labeled,  respec-
             tively,  by  “R2,α2,η2,s2”  to  “R1,α1,η1,s1”,  associate  a  factor U1δR1,R2 (1−δα1α2δη1η2δs1s2)  +
             U2P5i=0δR1,R2+Ci6zaM1.
        (c)Sum over internal degrees of freedom: lattice sites, orbital numbers, valleys, spins, and imaginary frequen-
             cies.
        (d)Finally, as explained in Ref. [292], multiply the expression by

                                                                 (−1)m+F
                                                                     βm  ,                                         (S3.8)
             whereF is the number of closedf-fermion loops, andm is the number of interaction vertices in the diagram
             (i.e., the order of the diagram). As a result of this rule, the two diagrams in Figs. S8(h) and S8(i) differ
             by an relative minus sign, as a result of the first one having a closed fermion loop.

Note that Eqs. (S3.21) and (S3.22) only involve a sequence of one-dimensional integrals (one to compute the auxiliary
functions in Eqs. (S3.23) and (S3.24) and one to compute the self-energy contributions from Eqs. (S3.21) and (S3.22)).
In contrast, Eqs. (S3.15) and (S3.21) involve a nested triple integral. As a result, Eqs. (S3.21) and (S3.22) are much
more amenable to numerical evaluation and, as we will show in Appendix [III4], can be used to self-consistently solve
the THF model within second-order perturbation theory. The total f-electron self-energy at the second-order level
can be found by summing the two contributions from Eqs. (S3.21) and (S3.22)

                                    Σf,(2) ω +i0+≡ Σf,(2a) ω +i0+ + Σf,(2b) ω +i0+,                    (S3.25)

which correspond, respectively, to the two diagrams from Fig. S10.


                             4.    The self-consistent problem and numerical implementation

  Having derived the second-order self-energy correction stemming from the HU1 and HU2 terms of the interaction
Hamiltonian, we now show how the THF model can be solved numerically in the symmetry-broken phase using
self-consistent second-order perturbation theory.
  For a given density matrixϱ (k) and a local (i.e., k-independent) second-order self-energy correction Σ(2) (ω +i0+),
the fully-interacting (up to second order in the HU1 and HU2 interactions and up to first order in all the other
interaction terms) retarded Green’s function of the system can be found from the Dyson equation
                                                  h ω +i0+ +µ 1−hMF (k)− Σ(2) ω +i0+i−1,                (S3.26)
                              G ω +i0+, k =

where the mean-field Hamiltonian hMF (k) is determined from the density matrix via Eq. (S2.24) or Eq. (S2.26).
At the same time,G (ω +i0+, k) determines the spectral function of the system via Eq. (S2.15), and, through the
                                                                                                                                             44

latter, the density matrix of the system ϱ (k) from Eq. (S2.17) as well as the second-order f-electron self-energy
Σf,(2) (ω +i0+) from Eqs. (S3.21) to (S3.25). Assuming that the c- and d-fermions have a constant broadening Γc,
the entire second-order self-energy correction Σ(2) (ω +i0+) can also be determined from
                                                       (
                           Σ(2)                          Σf,(2)(i−4)ηs;(i′−4)η′s′ (ω +i0+)    if    5≤i,i′≤ 6
                             iηs;i′η′s′ ω +i0+ =        −iΓcδii′δηη′δss′              otherwise    .             (S3.27)

  We employ Γc = 1 meV (Γc = 1.5 meV) for TBG (TSTG), which is kept constant during the iterative algorithm
described below9.  The broadening factor assigned to the c- and d-electrons is added to avoid numerical issues
associated with the matrix inversion used to compute the retarded Green’s function of the system from Eq. (S3.26).
If the dynamical self-energy of the c- and f-electrons is set to zero, thenG (ω +i0+, k) will diverge at frequencies
equal to the charge-one excitations at the edge of the moiré BZ, where the remote bands are mostly c-electron-like
in character (or in the case of TSTG at the Dirac cone energy and momenta).  Γc can alternatively be understood as
the small “+i0+” term added to the frequency (for thec- andd-electron block) when computing the retarded Green’s
function. No such factor is needed in thef-electron block, since the diagonal part of thef-electron self-energy already
contains a positive imaginary part. It is worth mentioning that since Γc is much smaller than any other energy-scaleno
of the problem (including Im         Σf,(2)αηs;αηs (ω +i0+)    around the f-electron excitation energies), the exact choice of Γc
is inconsequential, a fact which we checked numerically.
  Taken together Eqs. (S2.15), (S2.17), (S2.24), (S2.26) and (S3.21) to (S3.27) form a self-consistent system of integral
equations for the density matrixϱ (k) and the second-order local self-energy Σ(2) (ω +i0+). We solve the system using
the following iterative algorithm: first, we denote by ϱn (k) and Σ(2)n   (ω +i0+) the density matrix and second-order
local self-energy of the system at the n-th step of the algorithm. To obtain ϱn+1 (k) and Σ(2)n+1 (ω +i0+), we employ
the following sequence:
   1.From  ϱn (k), we determine the Hartree-Fock Hamiltonian of the system (i.e.  the first-order contribution to
       the self-energy) through Eqs. (S2.24) and (S2.26). From the Hartree-Fock Hamiltonian and Σ(2)n   (ω +i0+), we
       determine the retarded Green’s function of the systemG (ω +i0+, k) using Eq. (S3.26).
   2.We then determine the spectral function of the system  A (ω, k) through Eq. (S2.15). At this step, we also fix
       the chemical potential of the system by requiring that the desired filling ν0 obeys
                                                 X    X     Z ∞
                                      ν0 =   1N0                                                    2   .                     (S3.28)
                                                   k  i,η,s    −∞ dωnF (ω)Aiηs;iηs (ω, k)− 1

       We find that working at fixed filling is more stable numerically than working at a fixed chemical potential.
   3.Once the chemical potential  µ has been fixed, we determineϱn+1 (k) from the spectral function via Eq. (S2.17).

   4.Also from the spectral function, we compute    Σ(2)n+1 (ω +i0+) via Eqs. (S3.21) to (S3.25) and (S3.27).
  The iterations are repeated until convergence is achieved. Numerically, we employ a mesh of  48× 48 = 2304 k-
points. Along the discretized real frequency axis we use 212 ω-points. As shown in Eq. (S3.26), the algorithm entails
inverting  48× 48× 212 matrices at each step in order to obtain the retarded Green’s function. To accelerate the
code, we use the Intel MKL library. Furthermore, the continuous Fourier integrals over ω and λ from Eqs. (S3.21)
to (S3.24) are approximated by a Discrete Fourier Transformation (which is then computed using the Fast Fourier
transform algorithm) according to the method devised by Ref. [308]. Finally, we consider the solution to be converged
when the relative change in the density matrix and second-order self-energy are no larger than 5× 10−4 and 5× 10−3,
respectively. In practice, we also dampen any potential oscillations around the converged solution by linearly mixing
the n-th and (n + 1)-th solutions at each step.
  For the various correlated states from Table S1, the second-order self-consistent results can be obtained by starting
the iterative algorithm with the zero-temperature Hartree-Fock density matrix of the given integer-filled correlated
state. The initial guess for the second-order self-energy correction is simply given by a small broadening

                                                Σ(2)iηs;i′η′s′ ω +i0+ =−iΓcδii′δηη′δss′.                            (S3.29)







9 The fact that ΓTSTGc   /ΓTBGc       = 3/2≈√2 reflects the scaling of the TSTG band structure by a factor of approximately√2 relative to
  the TBG one [194].
                                                                                                                                              45

  Once the second-order self-consistent solution of a given correlated state at integer filling ν0 is obtained, the
symmetry-broken phases at small doping around ν0 can be approached by doping the integer-filled solution in small
increments. To be precise, we consider the solutions at fillings ν0 +nδν, where n∈ Z and|n|≤    12δν (i.e. the integer-
filled correlated phase is doped in increments of δν by at most 1/2 away from the integer filling). The solution at
ν0+nδν is found using the self-consistentϱ (k) and Σ(2) (ω +i0+) obtained forν0+(n−1)δν (ifn> 0) orν0+(n+1)δν
(if n< 0) as initial conditions. In practice we take δν = 1/50.


                     Appendix IV: The symmetric phase of the THF model beyond Hartree-Fock

  This appendix offers a detailed overview of the symmetric phase of the THF model and its characterization. To
effectively capture the correlation effects within this phase, we employ a combination of dynamical mean-field theory
(DMFT) [263, 264], Hartree-Fock theory, and iterated perturbation theory (IPT) [268–289]. After briefly introducing
the notation, our approach begins with a characterization of the THF model’s symmetric phase. Subsequently, we
review the DMFT method, focusing on its application to the THF model in conjunction with Hartree-Fock theory. We
then detail the IPT method, emphasizing its numerical implementation. Finally, we mention that solving the THF
model in the symmetric phase using the aforementioned techniques relies on several rather technical intermediate
results from many-body perturbation theory. Therefore, this appendix will present the methodology without proof,
with the more complex technical derivations being deferred to Appendix [V].


                                                       1.    Summary of notation

  Considering the technical depth of the discussions in this appendix and in Appendix [V], it will be practical to
begin with a summary of the functions and symbols that will be frequently used. Their definitions and the specific
equations where they are introduced are listed in Table S2. This concise notation summary is intended as a quick
reference to facilitate easier navigation and understanding of the subsequent sections. First time readers might choose
to skip this table and then consult it as needed.

                                             2.    The symmetric phase of the THF model

   The main focus of this appendix is the symmetric phase of the THF model. At low temperatures (usually below
10 K in experiments or DMFT simulations [4]) and around integer fillings, TBG and TSTG order, i.e. they form
phases that break some of the symmetries of the system. For example, the|ν =−2,K-IVC⟩ correlated phase from
Table S1 breaks (among other symmetries) the time-reversal, valley U (1), and spin SU (2) symmetries of the system.
On the other hand, at higher temperatures (i.e. above approximately 10 K), the system is likely to be in an unordered
                                                                                                                                           47

or symmetric phase.
  While the system is not ordered in the symmetric phase, interaction effects are still expected to play an important
role. This is because the relevant temperatures, which are high with respect to the order-disorder critical temperatures
(approximately 10 K)[4],arestillmuchlowerthanthecharacteristictemperatureoftheleadinginteractionscale(which
isoftheorderofhundredsofKelvin). Moreover, asopposedtotheorderedphasesdiscussedinAppendices[II]and[III],
for which the excitations’ dispersion was primarily determined by the static (Hartree-Fock) self-energy contribution,
while the dynamic one mainly affected their lifetimes, for the symmetric phases, the dynamical contribution to the
self-energy will play a significant role for both their lifetime and their dispersion (e.g., the Hubbard bands appearing
in the symmetric phase arise from the dynamic part of the self-energy) [4].
  In what follows, we will first discuss the constraints that various symmetries impose on the THF Green’s function
and associated quantities and then define and characterize the symmetric phase of the THF model.


                                 a.  Symmetry constraints on the THF interacting Green’s function

  We begin by considering a symmetry operation g, whose action on the THF fermions is given by

                                          gˆγ†k,i,η,sg−1 = X                          (−)gk,i′η′s′,                          (S4.1)
                                                            i′,η′,s′ [D(g)]i′η′s′;iηs ˆγ†

whereD(g) is the unitary representation matrix of g and (−)gk denotes the action of g on the Cartesian momentum
vector k. An additional minus sign, which was denoted by (−), is introduced if g is an antiunitary symmetry. In the
notation introduced in Appendix [II1], it is easy to show, using the cyclic property of the trace, as well as the fact
that g is a symmetry of the THF Hamiltonian (and hence commutes with it), that for any operatorO

                      ⟨O⟩ = Tr e−βKO = Tr ge−βKOg−1(∗) = Tr e−βKgOg−1(∗) =
gOg−1(∗),           (S4.2)

with  (∗) denoting complex conjugation for the cases when g is antiunitary. Through Eq. (S4.2), Eq. (S4.1) imposes
the following constraints on the non-interacting and interacting Green’s functions of the THF model

                 D−1(g)G0,(∗) (τ, (−)gk)D(g) =G0 (τ, k),  and  D−1(g)G(∗) (τ, (−)gk)D(g) =G (τ, k).       (S4.3)

In Eq. (S4.3), the constraint on the interacting Green’s functionG (τ, k) only holds for the cases when the symmetryg
is not spontaneously broken in the corresponding phase. Fourier-transforming in the Matsubara frequency according
to Eq. (S2.8), we find that

   D−1(g)G0,(∗) ((−)iωn, (−)gk)D(g) =G0 (iωn, k),  and  D−1(g)G(∗) ((−)iωn, (−)gk)D(g) =G (iωn, k).   (S4.4)

Using the spectral representation of the interacting Green’s function from Eq. (S2.16), as well as the Hermiticity of
the spectral function, one can easily show that
                                                         Z ∞      dω
                                           G† (z, k) =          z∗−ωA† (ω, k) =G (z∗, k),                          (S4.5)
                                                           −∞

and similarly for the non-interacting Green’s function. In turn Eq. (S4.5) allows us to recast Eq. (S4.4) to

       D−1(g)G0,(T) (iωn, (−)gk)D(g) =G0 (iωn, k),  and  D−1(g)G(T) (iωn, (−)gk)D(g) =G (iωn, k),      (S4.6)

which, upon analytical continuation becomes

                 D−1(g)G0,(T) (z, (−)gk)D(g) =G0 (z, k),  and  D−1(g)G(T) (z, (−)gk)D(g) =G (z, k),       (S4.7)

where the reader is reminded that the transposition and the minus sign in front of the momentum argument are only
to be added for the cases where g is antiunitary.
  In this appendix, we will find it useful to explicitly separate the static (i.e. Hartree-Fock) and dynamic contributions
to the self-energy and recast Dyson’s equation as

                                          G−1 (z, k) = G0 (z, k)−1−hMF (k)− Σ (z, k).                         (S4.8)
                                                                                                                                            48

In Eq. (S4.8), hMF (k) denote the static contribution to the self-energy and are first order in the interaction, while
Σ (z, k) is the dynamical self-energy contribution and incorporates the contributions which are beyond first order in
the interaction. Using Eq. (S4.7), we can directly show that:

                                               D−1(g)A(∗) (ω, (−)gk)D(g) =A (ω, k),                             (S4.9)
                                                   DT (g)ϱ(∗) ((−)gk)D∗(g) =ϱ (k),                              (S4.10)
                                               D−1(g)hMF(∗) ((−)gk)D(g) =hMF (k),                            (S4.11)
                                               D−1(g)Σ(T) (z, (−)gk)D(g) = Σ (z, k).                            (S4.12)

In proving Eq. (S4.9), we have additionally employed Eq. (S2.15). Eq. (S4.10) then follows by integrating the spectral
function according to Eq. (S2.17). Using Dyson’s equation from Eq. (S4.8), we can impose the symmetry constraints
on the THF model’s self-energy and show that Eqs. (S4.11) and (S4.12) have to hold independently, since hMF (k)
and Σ (z, k) are first order and beyond first order in the interaction.
  In this appendix, as in Appendix [III2], we will incorporate only f-electron correlation effects. To this end, we
particularize Eqs. (S4.9) to (S4.12) for the f-electron block. By Fourier-transforming the corresponding block of the
Green’s function and dynamical self-energy according to Eqs. (S3.3) and (S3.4), we directly obtain

                                               D−1f   (g)Gf,(T) (z,gR)Df(g) =Gf (z, R),                           (S4.13)
                                              D−1f   (g)Σf,(T) (z,gR)Df(g) = Σf (z, R),                           (S4.14)

where the unitary matrix Df(g) represents the f-electron block of the D(g) representation matrix

                                [Df(g)]αηs;α′η′s′ = [D(g)](α+4)ηs;(α′+4)η′s′,  for    1≤α,α′≤ 2.                 (S4.15)


                        b.  The symmetric phase of the THF model within DMFT and Hartree-Fock theory

  The interaction Hamiltonian of the THF model is given in Eqs. (S1.11) and (S1.22) and is also shown below

                           HI =HU1 +HU2 +HV  +HW +HJ +H ˜J +HK                           +HdV  +HcdV  +HfdW        ,             (S4.16)

where the terms in the parenthesis only appear in the case of TSTG. Due to their zero-kinetic energy and large onsite
Hubbard repulsion, thef-electrons are expected to be strongly correlated. Conversely, thec-electrons (andd-electrons
in TSTG) are highly dispersive, suggesting that they are only weakly correlated. Therefore, it is sufficient to treat the
interactions between the c-electrons (HV), as well as between the c- and f-electrons (HW +HJ +H ˜J +HK) at the
Hartree-Fock level. For TSTG, this also applies to HdV, HcdV , and HfdW . Finally, since the nearest-neighbor Hubbard
interaction between f-electrons (HU2) is much weaker than the on-site one, we expect the correlation physics to be
mostly driven by the HU1 term. As a result, we will also treat the HU2 term via Hartree-Fock theory.
  Therefore, in the symmetric phase, we will assume that the dynamical part of the f-electron interaction self-energy
arises exclusively from the HU1 term (with all the other terms being treated at the Hartree-Fock level). Compared
to the symmetry-broken calculations from Appendix [III], the HU2 term of the Hamiltonian is treated at the mean-
field level. In what follows, we will determine the dynamical f-electron self-energy using DMFT, as will be outlined
Appendix [IV3]. To do so, we will first approximate the THF Hamiltonian by decoupling all interactions except HU1
at the Hartree-Fock level and obtain
      HDMFT+HF = X             hMF′iηs;i′η′s′ (k) ˆγ†                      X      : ˆf†
                                                 k,i,η,sˆγk,i′,η′,s′ + U12          R,α,η,s ˆfR,α,η,s:: ˆf†R,α′,η′,s′ ˆfR,α′,η′,s′:.     (S4.17)
                        k,i,η,s                                          R,α,η,s
                        i′,η′,s′                                         α′,η′,s′

In Eq. (S4.17), hMF′ (k) is the quadratic mean-field Hamiltonian, which incorporates both the single-particle contri-
bution and all the THF interactions except HU1 decoupled at the Hartree-Fock level and whose expression will be
given in Eq. (S4.20).
  Because thef-electrons are dispersionless and have a large onsite repulsion, we expect their onsite inter-orbital, val-
ley, or spin correlations to dominate over the offsite ones. As a result, and in the same spirit as the last approximation
of Appendix [III1b], we will take the f-electron dynamical self-energy to be site-diagonal, i.e.

                                                 Σfαηs;α′η′s′ (iωn, R) = 0  if   R̸= 0,                             (S4.18)
                                                                                                                                            49

in the notation of Eq. (S3.4).  Moreover, within the symmetric phase, which is the focus of this appendix, the
ground state candidate (or thermal ensemble of low-energy states) preserves all the symmetries of the system, namely
moiré translation symmetry,C6z rotation symmetry, time-reversal symmetry, and SU (2)× SU (2) spin-valley rotation
symmetry [111, 113]. By imposing all these symmetries on thef-electron self-energy according to Eq. (S4.14), we can
show that site-diagonal self-energy ansatz from Eq. (S4.18) becomes

                                          Σfαηs;α′η′s′ (iωn, R) = ˜Σf (iωn)δαα′δηη′δss′δR,0,                        (S4.19)

In Eq. (S4.19), as well as throughout this appendix and Appendix [V], the diagonal entries of a matrix M are denoted
with a tilde as the scalar   ˜M.
  In addition to the approximation from Eq. (S4.19), we also expect the largest contribution to the f-electron Green’s
function to be site-diagonal. Such an approximation would be exact if the f-electrons were not hybridized with thec-
and/or d-electrons, as it happens in the zero-hybridization limit considered by Ref. [251]. In computing the Hartree-
Fock Hamiltonian hMF′ (k) from Eq. (S4.17), we will, therefore, ignore any terms that depend on the correlation
betweenf-electrons belonging to different sites. The only such contribution is the Fock term arising from HU2, which
will therefore be dropped in the symmetric phase calculations. As a result, the Hartree-Fock Hamiltonian matrix from
Eq. (S4.17) is related to the fully-decoupled one defined in Eqs. (S2.24) and (S2.26) for TBG and TSTG, respectively,
by subtracting from the latter the Hartree-Fock contribution of HU1, as well as the Fock contribution of HU2, i.e.
                       hMFiηs;i′η′s′ (k)−U1νfδii′δηη′δss′
                                     h                                              i
     hMF′iηs;i′η′s′ (k) =  +Pk′     U1 +U2P5n=0 cos ((k− k′)·Cn6zaM1)               ϱi′η′s′;iηs (k′),    if    5≤i,i′≤ 6      .    (S4.20)
                          hMFiηs;i′η′s′ (k),                                               otherwise

As a result, it is important to note that the f-electron block of hMF′ (k) is k-independent and proportional to the
identity matrix, i.e.

                           hMF′(α+4)ηs;(α′+4)η′s′ (k) =hMF′(α+4)ηs;(α′+4)η′s′ (0) =hMF′5+↑;5+↑ (0)δαα′δηη′δss′,             (S4.21)

where the last equality follows from Eq. (S4.11), in the symmetric phase.
  From Eq. (S4.13), we can also show that in the symmetric phase, the site-diagonal part of the f-electron Green’s
function is also diagonal in the f-electron indices. Using the notation from Eq. (S3.3), this is equivalent to
                                                                 R=0
                                          Gfαηs;α′η′s′ (iωn, R)         =  ˜Gf (iωn)δαα′δηη′δss′,                        (S4.22)


where  ˜Gf (iωn) is the site- and flavor-diagonal part of the f-electron Green’s function.  For convenience, we also
introduce the relative filling of the f-electrons in the system n, which can be related to the site- and flavor-diagonal
f-electron Green’s function by employing Eqs. (S2.15), (S2.17), (S2.19), (S3.3) and (S4.22)
                                                        X     Z ∞
                             n≡ νf + 4Nf    =     1N0Nf

                                                X     Z ∞

where Nf = 8 is the number of f-electron fermion flavors.

                                                            a.  The single-site problem

The three terms in Eq. (S4.27) correspond to the action of the f-electrons at the lattice site R0 (Sbaress  ), the action
of all the other degrees of freedom, (Scavity), and a term that couples the f-electrons at lattice site R0 with all the
other fermionic degrees of freedom (∆S). The system formed by all the fermions of the model except the f-electrons
at lattice site R0 is termed the “cavity”. The partition function of the lattice problem from Eq. (S4.24) can then be
rewritten as                                        Z                         Z

  We now formally integrate out all the f-, c-, or d-electrons of the system except  ˆf†α,η,s. To do so, we can interpret

This allows us to formally obtain

Moreover, we note that the cavity system with the  ˆf†α,η,s fermion source field (for which the action is given byScavity +

spin-valley rotation symmetry (these symmetries are preserved even when theSbaress   part of the action is removed). As

is diagonal

                                                  Gcavityαηs;α′η′s′ (τ) =  ˜Gcavity (τ)δαα′δηη′δss′.                            (S4.38)

Finally, we note that the  ˜Gcavity (τ) function is a complicated object that depends on the fully-interacting Green’s
function of the cavity system, which cannot be (in general) computed explicitly.
   To move forward, we use one of the key simplifying assumptions of the DMFT method and ignore all the terms
in Eq. (S4.35) that are not quadratic in the  ˆf†α,η,s fermions (where quadratic term corresponds to the n = 1 term),
which allows us to approximate
