  Eq. (S4.43) forms the central focus of the DMFT method. The reader is reminded that Eq. (S4.43) was obtained
by integrating out all the fermions except the f-electrons from the lattice site R0. Any terms other than quadratic
in the  ˆf†α,η,s fermions arising from the integration procedure were then ignored. We also note that the expression
of G−10   (τ) from Eq. (S4.42) is only of formal importance: in practice G−10   (τ) cannot be computed this way, as it
requires knowledge on the exact solution of the cavity system. Instead, G−10   (τ) needs to be obtained self-consistently,
as we will show in Appendix [IV3b].
                                                                                                                                           54

                                                         b.  The DMFT solution

  Within DMFT, for a givenG0 (τ) (which will be determined self-consistently, as shown later), one solves the action
Sss via some exact or approximate method and obtains the interacting Green’s function for the f-electrons of the
single-site model
                                                              D                         Ess,                             (S4.52)
                                                 G (τ) =−      Tτ ˆfα,η,s (τ) ˆf†α,η,s (0)

where both the imaginary time evolution and the thermodynamic averaging are done within the single-site action
from Eq. (S4.43). OnceG (τ) is computed, the self-energy of the single-sitef-electrons can be obtained (by definition)
from G (τ) and G0 (τ) using Dyson’s equation,

                                 U1    nss− 12     (Nf− 1) + Σss (iωn) =G−10   (iωn)−G−1 (iωn),                  (S4.53)

where the Fourier transformations of G(τ) and G0(τ) Green’s function in the Matsubara frequency are defined anal-
ogously to Eq. (S2.8). The first and second terms on the left hand side of Eq. (S4.53) are, respectively, the static
(Hartree-Fock) and dynamic contributions to the self-energy of the single-site model, with
                                                                 D ˆf†α,η,s ˆfα,η,sEss.                                   (S4.54)
                                                         nss≡

  In DMFT, there are two further assumptions (both of which are exactly satisfied for the infinite-dimensional
Hubbard model) [263], which simultaneously (1) provide a solution to the original lattice problem from Eq. (S4.17),
and (2) enable one to compute G0 (τ):
    • First, we take the f-electron self-energy of the lattice problem to be k-independent and equal to the f-electron
      self-energy of the single-site model. Specifically, letting
                                                           (˜Σf (iωn)δii′δηη′δss′    if  i = 5, 6
                                     Σiηs;i′η′s′ (iωn) =     0                    otherwise ,                     (S4.55)

      with
                                                          ˜Σf (iωn) = Σss (iωn),                                    (S4.56)

      the k-dependent Green’s function of the lattice problem can be calculated via Dyson’s equation
                                                                                                                   −1
                      G (iωn, k) =     (iωn +µ) 1−hMF′ (k)−U1              nss− 12     (Nf− 1) 1f− Σ (iωn)            ,          (S4.57)

      where 1f is the identity matrix in the f-electron block
                                                              (
                                             [1f]iηs;i′η′s′ =   δii′δηη′δss′    if  i = 5, 6
                                                                0            otherwise .                          (S4.58)

      Note that there is no dynamical contribution to the c- and/or d-electrons’ self-energy, as the corresponding
      interaction terms are treated at the Hartree-Fock level in Eq. (S4.17).
    • Second, we take the full Green’s function of the single-site model to be equal to the site-diagonal interacting
      Green’s function of the lattice model [263], which was defined in Eq. (S4.22),

                                                           G (iωn) =  ˜Gf (iωn).                                    (S4.59)

      As a result of Eq. (S4.59), we must have that n = nss. This implies that for a given lattice Green’s function
      G (iωn, k) and f-electron self-energy  ˜Σf (iωn) (assumed to be k-independent), the non-interacting single-site
      Green’s function G0 (iωn) can be recovered from Eqs. (S4.53), (S4.56) and (S4.59),
                                                  ˜Gf (iωn) −1 +U1
                                G−10   (iωn) =                            n− 12     (Nf− 1) + ˜Σf (iωn).                 (S4.60)

      It is Eq. (S4.60), as opposed to Eq. (S4.42), which is used in practice to obtain G0 (iωn).
                                                                                                                                         55

  Taken together, Eqs. (S4.57) and (S4.60) form a self-consistent problem, as illustrated in Fig. 2. The two quantities
G0 (iωn) and Σ0 (iωn) are interdependent and need to be determined at the same time so as to satisfy both Eq. (S4.53)
– which implies that  ˜Σf (iωn) = Σss (iωn) is the exact self-energy of the single-site problem – and Eq. (S4.60) – which
relates back the dynamical f-electron self-energy to the non-interacting Green’s function of the single-site problem.
  In our problem, as we also have a Hartree-Fock contribution stemming from the interactions other than HU1,
we will vary the density matrix ϱ (k), the Green’s function G0 (τ), and the dynamical f-electron self energy until
all the self-consistent equations, including Eq. (S2.17) which relates the density matrix back to the lattice Green’s
function, are satisfied. The detailed description and implementation of the self-consistent procedure will be given in
Appendix [IV4b].


                                       c.  Analytical properties of the G0(z), G(z), and Σss(z)

  Bona fide Green’s functions admit a spectral representation. For example, the full (i.e. unapproximated) Green’s
function of the THF model admits the spectral representation from Eq. (S2.16).  The proof and implication of a
spectral representation are discussed at lengths in Ref. [298, 299] and will not be repeated here.
  On the other hand, the “Green’s functions” of the single-site model and the corresponding dynamical self-energies
are more complicated objects and it is not immediately obvious whether they admit a spectral representation. Setting
aside issues related to the uniqueness of the DMFT solution [309, 310], Ref. [311] has shown that if the DMFT
self-consistent cycle in Fig. 2 is solved iteratively starting from a guess of the lattice Green’s function G (iωn, k)
which admits a spectral representation, then at each iteration, G0(z), G(z), and  Σss(z) will all admit a spectral
representation
                   Z ∞    ρ0(ω)                                                                      Z ∞
          G0(z)=          z−ω dω,  with  ρ0(ω)≡− 1π ImG0 ω +i0+,  ρ0(ω)≥0,
                   Z ∞−∞                                                                              Z ∞−∞ρ0(ω) dω=1        (S4.61)
           G(z)=          ρ(ω)

                     −∞   z−ω dω,  with  ρ(ω)≡− 1π ImG ω +i0+,   ρ(ω)≥0,                              −∞ρ(ω) dω=1,       (S4.62)

and similarly for the self-energy
                                          Z ∞     dω
                          Σss(z) =− 1π          z−ω Im Σss ω +i0+,  with    Im Σss ω +i0+≤ 0.             (S4.63)
                                           −∞
Rigorous mathematical results have also been proven in Ref. [288, 289]. In what follows, we will take Eqs. (S4.61)
to (S4.63) to hold for our DMFT self-consistent solution. As a corollary of Eqs. (S4.61) and (S4.62), we have that

                                               G0(z) = 1z,  G(z) = 1z,  as  z→∞,                            (S4.64)

while

                                                      Σss(z)∝ 1z,  as  z→∞,                                 (S4.65)

where the proportionality constant will be derived in Appendix [V2].  Additionally, it is easy to see that from
Eqs. (S4.61) to (S4.63), that G0(z), G(z), and Σss(z) are analytical above and below the real axis, with

                                   G0 (z∗) =G∗0 (z),  G (z∗) =G∗ (z),    Σss (z∗) = Σ∗ss (z).                    (S4.66)

  ThefinalquestionconcernstheexistenceofaspectralrepresentationforthelatticeGreen’sfunctionG (iωn, k)(given
that it is not the exact interacting Green’s function of the THF model, but rather some Green’s function constructed
with the DMFT f-electron self-energy). Ref. [311] also proves that if Σss (z) admits a spectral representation akin to
the one in Eq. (S4.63), then
                                                                                                            −1
                           G (z, k) =    (z +µ) 1−hMF′ (k)−U1             n− 12     (Nf− 1) 1f− Σ (z)           ,              (S4.67)

will also admit the usual spectral representation of a Green’s function from Eqs. (S2.15) and (S2.16). As a result, we
will assume that all the Green’s function computed within the DMFT and Hartree-Fock theory will obey the usual
analytic properties and have bona fide spectral representations.
                                                                                                                                           56

                                4.    Solving the DMFT and Hartree-Fock problem with IPT

   As we described in the Appendix [IV3], within the DMFT approximation, the lattice model is mapped to a single-
site problem described by the action Sss introduced in Eq. (S4.43). The dynamical self-energy of the lattice problem
is then approximated by the dynamical self-energy of the single-site problem, which still needs to be solved exactly.
Throughout this work, we will employ the approximate IPT method [268, 270–281, 283, 284, 286, 287] to obtain the
dynamical self-energy of the single-site problem. Compared to exact quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) methods [3, 4],
this method offers two significant advantages: time-efficiency and, as we will see, the avoidance of numerical analytical
continuation from Matsubara to real frequency (which is a numerically ill-posed problem). In this section, we will
provide an overview of IPT method, stating all the results without proof, with all the detailed derivations being
relegated to Appendix [V].


                                                     a.  Overview of the IPT method

   To start with, we introduce a modified non-interacting Green’s function for the single-site problem at a different
chemical potential  ˜µ

                                                    G˜µ0 (iωn) =               1G−10   (iωn)−µ + ˜µ,                               (S4.68)

together with its spectral representation11
                                                    ω +i0+,  such that  G˜µ                 Z ∞     dω
                           ρ˜µ0 (ω)≡− 1π ImG˜µ0                                    0 (z) =         z−ωρ˜µ0 (ω).             (S4.69)
                                                                                              −∞
As we explain in Appendix [V3e], the so-called fictitious chemical potential  ˜µ can be thought of as variational
parameter which will be fixed in such a way so as to render the IPT approximation for the dynamical self-energy as
close as possible to the exact result.
   The first idea of the IPT method is to solve the single-site problem to second order in perturbation theory using
the G˜µ0 (iωn) propagator. The corresponding diagonal part of the dynamical self-energy is given by
                                                       Z ∞        Z ∞        Z ∞
                  ˜Σf,(2) ω +i0+ =U21 (Nf− 1)                                           0 (ω1)ρ˜µ0 (ω2)ρ˜µ0 (ω3)
                                                        −∞ dω1     −∞ dω2      −∞ dω3ρ˜µ
                                         × nF (ω1) (1−nF (ω2))nF (ω3) + (1−nF (ω1))nF (ω2) (1−nF (ω3))ω−ω1 +ω2−ω3 +i0+              .       (S4.70)

However, for reasons that will be explained in detail in Appendix [V], within IPT,  ˜Σf,(2) (ω +i0+) is not used directly
asthedynamicalself-energyofthesingle-siteproblem. Instead, thelatterisapproximatedbythefollowing interpolated
ansatz

                                        ˜Σf,Int ω +i0+ =     a˜Σf,(2) (ω +i0+)
                                                               1−b (ω +i0+) ˜Σf,(2) (ω +i0+).                       (S4.71)
The constant a and the function b (ω +i0+) are respectively chosen such that the interpolated self-energy
˜Σf,Int (ω +i0+) obeys the correct (exact) asymptotic form in the infinite frequency (ω → ∞) limit and reduces
to the exact analytical atomic limit [291] expression  ˜Σf,At (ω +i0+) in the U1 →∞ limit (the atomic limit will
be briefly discussed above Eq. (S4.75) and then solved in Appendix [V1]). Additionally, it can also be shown that
˜Σf,Int (ω +i0+) reduces to the second-order perturbation result in the weakly interacting limit (U1→ 0). Because
˜Σf,Int (ω +i0+) reproduces the exact results in both the large (U1→∞) and small (U1→ 0) interaction limits, while
preserving the exact ω→∞ asymptotic form, the interpolated self-energy from Eq. (S4.71) is expected to correctly
capture the correlation physics of the f-electrons even in the intermediate coupling regime.
   For a given chemical potential µ and fictitious chemical potential  ˜µ, the constant a of Eq. (S4.71) is given by

                                               a = n + (Nf− 2)⟨nn⟩ss− (Nf− 1)n2         ,                            (S4.72)

Finally, we note that the fictitious chemical potential  ˜µ is fixed by ensuring that the f-electron filling of the non-

detail in Appendix [V3e].


                                    b.  The self-consistent problem and numerical implementation

  In Appendix [IV4a], we have outlined the IPT method by effectively listing a series of equations that the dynamical
self-energy solution needs to satisfy at a given chemical potential µ. All these equations need to be concurrently
satisfied with the DMFT self-consistent conditions from Eqs. (S4.57) and (S4.60), along with the Hartree-Fock ones
from Eqs. (S2.17), (S4.20) and (S4.57).
                                                                                                                                               58

   Because the resulting system of equations is nonlinear, it cannot be solved directly, and its solution must be
constructed numerically in an iterative fashion. To do so, we first note that at a given filling, the symmetric solution
of the THF model is fully specified by the density matrix and the k-independent dynamical self-energy of the system.
Indeed, the retarded THF Green’s function corresponding to a certain density matrix ρ (k) and to a dynamical
self-energy correction Σ (ω +i0+) can be found from Dyson’s equation to be
                                   ω +i0+ +µ 1−hMF′ (k)−U1
           G ω +i0+, k =                                                    n− 12     (Nf− 1) 1f− Σ ω +i0+ −1              ,        (S4.81)

with the density-matrix-dependent mean-field Hamiltonian hMF′ (k) from Eq. (S4.20) containing all the interactions
terms besides HU1 decoupled at the mean-field level. As mentioned above Eq. (S4.20), in the symmetric phase, we
ignore the Fock contribution stemming from HU2, which implies that hMF′ (k) only depends on the total f-electron
filling and not on the detailed momentum dependence of thef-electron block of the density matrix12. For the purpose
of this calculation, the latter can therefore be taken without loss of generality to be k-independent [as well as flavor
and site diagonal, as implied by Eq. (S4.22)], i.e.

                                      ρiηs;i′η′s′ (k) =    n− 12     δii′δηη′δss′,  for    5≤i,i′≤ 6,                    (S4.82)

in the notation introduced in Appendix [II1]. At the same time, the dynamical part of the self-energy is given by(˜Σf,Int (ω +i0+)δii′δηη′δss′    if    5≤i,i′≤ 6

                            Σiηs;i′η′s′ ω +i0+ =          −iΓcδii′δηη′δss′              otherwise    ,              (S4.83)

where, identically to Appendix [III], we have ascribed a constant broadening  Γc to the c- and d-fermions (with
Γc  =  1 meV for TBG and  Γc  =  1.5 meV for TSTG). The dynamical self-energy of the f-electrons is given by the
interpolated self-energy from Eq. (S4.71). The choice of broadening factors for the c- and/ord-electrons was justified
around Eq. (S3.27).
   Fig. S11 illustrates the iteration process used to construct the symmetric state solution. Just as in Appendix [III4],
we letϱm (k) and Σm (ω +i0+) be the density matrix and dynamical self-energy of the system at them-th step of the
algorithm. We also let  ˜Σf,Intm    (ω +i0+) = [Σm (ω +i0+)](α+4)ηs;(α+4)ηs denote the interpolated dynamical f-electron
self energy at step m. We postpone the discussion on how ϱ (k) and Σ (ω +i0+) are initialized to the end of this
section. To obtain ϱm+1 (k) and Σm+1 (ω +i0+), we employ the following sequence (note that the first two steps are
identical to the algorithm used to obtain the second-order self-consistent self-energy from Appendix [III4]):
    1.From  ϱm (k), we determine the Hartree-Fock Hamiltonian hMF′ (k) (i.e. the first-order contribution to the self-
       energy) through Eqs. (S2.24), (S2.26) and (S4.20). From the Hartree-Fock Hamiltonian and Σm (ω +i0+), we
       determine the retarded Green’s function of the systemG (ω +i0+, k) using Eq. (S4.81).
    2.We then determine the spectral function of the system  A (ω, k) through Eq. (S2.15). At this step, we also fix
       the chemical potential µ of the system by requiring that the desired filling ν0 obeys Z ∞
                                       ν0 =   1    X   X                                                  .                     (S4.84)
                                              N0    k  i,η,s    −∞ dωnF (ω)Aiηs;iηs (ω, k)− 12

       We find that working at fixed filling is more stable numerically (i.e. the solution converges within a smaller
       number of iterations) than working at a fixed chemical potential.
    3.Once the chemical potential  µ has been fixed, we determine the new density matrix ϱ′m+1 (k) from the spectral
       function via Eq. (S2.17).  Generally, the new density matrix ϱ′m+1 (k) is only approximately symmetric and
       k-independent in the f-electron block 13. Therefore, we symmetrize it by computing the relative f-electron
       filling n,
                                          n = 1               X    X    ϱ′m+1 (k)
                                                2 +     1N0Nf                        (α+4)ηs;(α+4)ηs,                       (S4.85)
                                                               k  α,η,s





12 This is consistent with our approximation in Eq. (S4.20), under which we ignore any contributions to the Hartree-Fock Hamiltonian
   arising on the correlation between f-electron belonging to different sites.
13 With infinite precision arithmetic, ϱ′m+1 (k) will inherit the symmetries of the Green’s functionG  ω +i0+. The latter is computed
   from the Hartree-Fock matrix of the symmetric phase and a symmetric self-energy, and hence will obey all the symmetries of TBG or
   TSTG. However, finite precision arithmetic causes small symmetry-breaking errors which, over the course of many iterations, might lead
   to the self-consistent solution not obeying the all the symmetries of the system. To avoid these numerical instabilities, we re-symmetrize
   the density matrix at every step.

   6.Finally, we use the second-order self-energy correction     ˜Σf,(2) (ω +i0+) to construct the new diagonal part of

      self-energy from Eq. (S4.83).
  The algorithm is iterated until convergence is achieved, where convergence is assessed by the same criteria as in
Appendix [III4]. We use the same k- and ω-point discretizations as in Appendix [III4]. The slowest part of the
algorithm is constructing the retarded Green’s function of the systemG (ω +i0+, k) using Eq. (S4.81), as that entails
numerous matrix inversions (one for each k and ω). We improve the efficiency of our algorithm by only computing
G (ω +i0+, k) for one spin sector and for one sixth of the BZ and then recovering the other matrix components
and k-points using the SU (2) spin and C6z rotation symmetries. As in Appendix [III4], we also efficiently compute
the Fourier integral of Eq. (S4.70) by means of a Fast Fourier transformation [308].  Any potential oscillations
in the self-consistent iterations are dampened by mixing the old and updated solution at every iteration: at the
end of the self-consistent cycle, we admix the old solution to the new one  ˜Σf,Intm+1 (ω +i0+)→ α˜Σf,Intm+1 (ω +i0+) +
(1−α) ˜Σf,Intm    (ω +i0+) and ϱm+1 (k) → αϱm+1 (k) + (1−α)ϱm (k), where α is a random number α ∈  [0.1, 0.5]
independently drawn at each iteration.
  To obtain results in the symmetric phase, we always start from charge neutrality and move towards doping the
remote bands in increments of δν  =   150. To obtain the charge-neutral solution (corresponding to the target filling
ν0 = 0), we take the following initial conditions for the density matrix and the dynamical self-energy:
                       Σiηs;i′η′s′ ω +i0+ =−iΓcδii′δηη′δss′  and  ϱiηs;i′η′s′ (k) = 0,  for all  i,i′,           (S4.89)
the first of which entails a constant broadening factor for all electron species.
  Once the self-consistent symmetric solution at charge neutrality is constructed, we obtain the solutions at ν0 =mδν
(where m∈ Z and 1≤ m≤  4.5δν ) incrementally using the self-consistent density matrix and dynamical self-energy
obtained at filling ν0 = (m− 1)δν as initial condition.


                                               5.    Benchmarking the IPT method

  In Section IVB, we benchmark the IPT method against a continuous-time quantum Monte Carlo (CT-QMC)
impurity solver. Here we outline the details of the latter simulations. In all CT-QMC runs, following Ref. [136], we
omit the H ˜J and HK interaction terms from Eqs. (S1.17) and (S1.18), respectively. As reviewed in Appendix [IV3],
DMFTcapturesthelocalcorrelationsgeneratedbytheonsiteinteractionHU1, butitcannotdirectlyincorporateoffsite
interactions such as the nearest-neighbor f-f repulsion HU2 or two-body operators involving momentum-dependent
c-electrons, including the density-density interactions HW, HV, and the magnetic exchange HJ.
  To handle the interaction Hamiltonian from Eq. (S1.11) within DMFT, we adopt the combined DMFT and Hartree-
Fock approach described in Appendix [IV3], where HU1 is treated dynamically and all other terms are mean-field
decoupled. At each DMFT iteration, the non-interacting Hamiltonian from Eq. (S1.1) is supplemented by HU1 and
a Hartree-Fock term of the type in Eq. (S2.22), but which only arises from HU2 and HV (both treated only at the
Hartree level), as well as HW and HJ (treated at the Hartree-Fock level). Explicitly, the interacting Hamiltonian
reads
                                                       X      ˆf†                               X

                           + X       Waνf ˆc†k,a,η,sˆck,a,η,s + X      WaOaηs;aηs ˆf†R,α,η,s ˆfR,α,η,s

                           −X             Wa′Oa′η′s′;(α+4)ηs ˆf†k,α,η,sˆck,a′,η′,s′ +h.c.
                               k  α,η,s
                                  a′η′s′
                                                                                                                                           61
                                X     X      h

is the onsite density matrix, updated at every DMFT iteration. Consistent with Ref. [136], we neglect the Fock term
of HV for these simulations (and in the corresponding IPT calculations used for comparison).  We also find that
including HJ has negligible influence above the ordering temperature (approximately 10 K).
  The CT-QMC simulations are performed with the DMFT-CTHYB solver w2dynamics [312, 313].  We set
β−1 = 1 meV, above the ordering temperature [4], and carry out fixed-density simulations for−1≤ν≤ +1 around
charge neutrality, adjusting the chemical potential dynamically within the DMFT loop to match the target filling.
Numerical parameters follow closely those of Ref. [4]: runs are executed on 144 CPU cores, with  Nmeas = 1.5× 105
measurements per core per DMFT step, and  Ncorr  =  1500 updates per core between measurements to suppress
autocorrelation effects. A total of  Nwarmup = 2· 105 thermalization steps per core are performed prior to measure-
ments. Analytical continuation is carried out starting from the final DMFT iteration using the MaxEnt method in
the ana_cont package [314].



                         Appendix V: Details on the IPT method as applied to the THF model

  This appendix provides the full derivation of all intermediate results used in Appendix [IV], where we discussed the
symmetric phase of the THF model and outlined its solution via a combination of DMFT and Hartree-Fock theory.
In particular, we previously introduced the IPT method as a tool to solve the DMFT equations, presenting only
the algorithmic steps without proof. Here, we supply the complete analytical derivations underpinning the identities
employed there. We begin by defining and solving the atomic limit of the THF model. Next, we derive two exact
properties of the dynamical self-energy in the symmetric phase, which are essential to the IPT framework. Finally,
we present a detailed derivation of the IPT ansatz for the dynamical self-energy.



                                             1.    The atomic limit of the THF model

  In this section, we consider the atomic problem for the THF f-electrons and derive the dynamical part of the
corresponding atomic self-energy. In the atomic limit, the f-electrons at different lattice sites are decoupled from
one another and also decoupled from the c- or d-electrons. Because in this limit, the f-electrons at a given lattice
site are not hybridizing or interacting with any other fermions, we can study the atomic problem via the following
Hamiltonian
               HAt = U1       X     : ˆf†α,η,s ˆfα,η,s:: ˆf†
                         2                           α′,η′,s′ ˆfα′,η′,s′:
                             α,η,s
                            α′,η′,s′
                     = U1          X           ˆf†α,η,s ˆfα,η,s ˆf†                             X     ˆf†α,η,s ˆfα,η,s + N2fU1
                         2                                   α′,η′,s′ ˆfα′,η′,s′− (Nf− 1)U12                             8  ,      (S5.1)
                            (α,η,s)̸=(α′,η′,s′)                                                 α,η,s

which is just the HU1 interaction Hamiltonian defined for a single-site. In Eq. (S5.1), Nf = 8 denotes the number of
f-fermion flavors and we have suppressed the position indices of the f-electron operators, as HAt is defined only for
a single-site.

                                          b.  Analytical approximation in the βU1→∞ limit

   TheexpressioninEq.(S5.22)isnotparticularlyilluminating. Toobtainananalyticalformulaforthedynamicalpart
of the atomic self-energy  ˜ΣAt (iωn), we can consider the βU1→∞ limit (i.e., the low-temperature limit) [280, 291].
Starting from Eq. (S5.12), we determine the ratio between the probabilities of the system having m1 andm2 particles
(with m1̸=m2) to be
                            pm1                                                U1
                            pm2  = m2! (Nf−m2)!m1! (Nf−m1)! expβ (m2−m1)         2  (m1 +m2−Nf)−µ                .              (S5.23)

For generic values of the chemical potential, U12  (m1 +m2−Nf)−µ will never be zero, meaning that one of the
probabilitiespm will be exponentially (inβU1) larger than all the other ones, which implies through the first equality
of Eq. (S5.18) that pm≈ 1, and through the second equality of Eq. (S5.18) that the system will be at some integer
filling (i.e., nAtNf  = m∈ N). For the system to be at a generic fractional filling, at least two of the probabilities
pm should be non-vanishing and, in this sense, have comparable values (as we show later, exactly two probabilities
have comparable values). Assume without loss of generality that  0≤ m1 < m2≤ Nf are the occupation numbers
corresponding to the largest two probabilitiespm1 andpm2 and thatpm1 andpm2 are both non-vanishing (pm1∼pm2).

Using Eqs. (S5.13) and (S5.20), we find that exactly at the integer filling, the dynamical self-energy of the atomic
problem is given by
                                     ˜ΣAt (iωn)≈   1        U21r (Nf−r)

   Finally, we note that the only approximation in Eqs. (S5.29) and (S5.37) is βU1→∞, meaning that the corre-
sponding expressions become exact in the limit of zero temperature.

                                               d.  The single-site model in the atomic limit

  By definition, in the atomic limit, the f-electrons at a given site are decoupled from all the other fermionic species
of the problem. As such, the latter cavity degrees of freedom can be trivially integrated out resulting in the following
single-site action
                                                                                                                                      
                 Z β      X                                               X                                                          
        SAtss  =                 ˆf†α,η,s (τ) (∂τ−µ) ˆfα,η,s (τ) + U12            : ˆf†α,η,s (τ) ˆfα,η,s (τ):: ˆf†α′,η′,s′ (τ) ˆfα′,η′,s′ (τ):
                  0   dτ   α,η,s                                           α,η,s
                                                                          α′,η′,s′
                 Z β     "X                                                    #
              =                  ˆf†α,η,s (τ) (∂τ−µ) ˆfα,η,s (τ) +HAt (τ)        .                                      (S5.40)
                  0   dτ   α,η,s

In the atomic limit, one can therefore identify

                           G0 (iωn)→  ˜GAt,0 (iωn),  G (iωn)→  ˜GAt (iωn),    Σss (iωn)→ ˜ΣAt (iωn),             (S5.41)
which also exactly satisfy the DMFT self-consistency conditions from Eqs. (S4.56) and (S4.59).


                    2.    Exact properties of the dynamical self-energy of the single-site THF model

  In this section, we derive two exact properties of the dynamical part of the self-energy of the single-site THF model
introduced in Appendix [IV3]. We will employ these two properties to show that the interpolated dynamical self-
energy  ˜Σf,Int (iωn) postulated in Eq. (S4.71) and further elaborated upon in Appendix [V3] reproduces the correct
asymptotic form of the exact dynamical self-energy  ˜Σf (iωn) as iωn→∞. After briefly summarizing the results of
this section, we will cast the single-site problem from the Lagrangian formulation of Eq. (S4.43) to an equivalent
Hamiltonian description. The latter will be employed to prove the exact properties of the dynamical self-energy of
the single-site model.


                                                                  a.  Results

  Given the technical nature of this section, we begin by presenting the main results which will be derived therein.
The ω→∞ asymptotic form of the exact dynamical self-energy of the single-site model defined in Eq. (S4.53) will
be shown to be given by
                                                                                                                   1
                       Σss ω +i0+ = U21 (Nf− 1)nss + (Nf− 2)⟨nn⟩ss− (Nf− 1)n2ssω                                        +Oω2,          (S5.42)

where nss and⟨nn⟩ss are the relative f-electron occupation and the correlator between the occupation of different
f-electron flavors in the single-site model.  As  Σss (ω +i0+) is the exact, rather than the second-order dynamical
                                                                                                                                               68

self-energy, it generically contains terms which are proportional to higher powers of U1, but its leading ω→∞ term
is proportional to U21. The two quantities nss and⟨nn⟩ss were introduced in Eqs. (S4.54) and (S4.74), respectively,
and also defined in Eqs. (S5.14) and (S5.15) for the atomic limit. In this section, we also show that for the single-site
model in the general case, the⟨nn⟩ss correlator can be related back to the single-site interacting Green’s function via
the second of the following equations
                D ˆf†α,η,s ˆfα,η,sEss =Z ∞
         nss≡
                                      −∞ dωnF (ω)ρ (ω),                                                     (S5.43)Z ∞
                D ˆf†α,η,s ˆfα,η,s ˆf†         Ess =n2ss−           1
     ⟨nn⟩ss≡                   α′,η′,s′ ˆfα′,η′,s′           πU1 (Nf− 1)
                                                                               −∞ dω ImΣss ω +i0+G ω +i0+	nF (ω),    (S5.44)
for any (α,η,s)̸= (α′,η′,s′). In Eq. (S5.44), we have used the interacting spectral function for the single-site model

                                                                                              Z ∞     dω

                                                                                               −∞

where the interacting Green’s function of the single-site model G (iωn) was defined in Eq. (S4.52).  As we show
explicitly in Appendix [V2d], requiring that  Σss (ω +i0+) has the exact ω→∞ asymptotic form, as imposed by
Eqs. (S5.42) and (S5.44) is equivalent to requiring that the first three moments of the interacting spectral function of
the single-site model [which will be defined in Eq. (S5.67)] are exactly reproduced [272].


                                      b.  The single-site problem in the Hamiltonian formulation

  As explained in Appendix [IV3a], within DMFT [263, 293], the dynamical part of the lattice self-energy  ˜Σf (iωn) is
site-diagonal and identical to Σss (iωn), the dynamical self-energy computed from the single-site action of Eq. (S4.43)
and repeated here for convenience,
                                         Z β      Z β
                               Sss =−                             ˆf†α,η,s (τ)G−10   (τ−τ′) ˆfα,η,s (τ′)
                                           0   dτ   0   dτ′ Xα,η,s
                                      Z β            X
                                   +            2           : ˆf†α,η,s (τ) ˆfα,η,s (τ):: ˆf†α′,η′,s′ (τ) ˆfα′,η′,s′ (τ):.
                                        0   dτ U1   α,η,s
                                                   α′,η′,s′

Sss wasobtainedfromtheHamiltonianofEq.(S4.17)byintegratingoutallthehybridizationandmean-fieldinteraction
terms between the eightf-fermions at a given site and the rest of the electrons (including all thec- and/ord-electrons),
and dropping the higher-order terms (which would give a contribution to the single-site action beyond the fermion
bilinear term). The Green’s functionG0 (τ) appearing in Eq. (S4.43) is thus not the non-interactingf-electron Green’s
function of the lattice problem, but rather an effective Green’s function that contains information about all the other
electronic degrees of freedom that were integrated out [269].
  For the purpose of deriving Eqs. (S5.42) and (S5.44), we will find it easier to work within the Hamiltonian, rather
than the Lagrangian formulation of the single-site problem. Note that the former is not trivial to obtain, since the
single-particle term of the action takes the retarded form
                                          Z β     Z β
                                       −                          ˆf†α,η,s (τ)G−10   (τ−τ′) ˆfα,η,s (τ′),                      (S5.45)
                                           0   dτ   0   dτ′ Xα,η,s

which differs from the canonical form−Rβ0  dτPα,η,s  ˆf†α,η,s (τ) (∂τ +ϵf) ˆfα,η,s (τ) of a non-interacting fermion.
  To obtain the Hamiltonian of the single-site problem, we first define the ∆ (iωn) hybridization field

                                                     ∆ (iωn)≡iωn +µ−G−10   (iωn).                               (S5.46)

                                Sss =X      X     ˆf†α,η,s (iωn) (−iωn−µ + ∆ (iωn)) ˆfα,η,s (iωn)

Up to the presence of the hybridization term ∆ (iωn), the kinetic term of Eq. (S5.47) resembles the canonical form
of the action of a non-interacting fermion. The iωn-dependent hybridization function can be removed by introducing
additional fermionic degrees of freedom. This can be shown by employing the spectral representation of ∆ (iωn),

                                                       ρ∆(ω) =− 1π ∆ ω +i0+.                                 (S5.50)

The mathematically rigorous proof for the existence of a spectral representation for ∆ (iωn) can be found in Ref. [311],
where the analytical properties of G(z) and G0(z) from Appendix [IV3c] are also proved. In fact, from Eq. (S4.64),, with ∆0∈ R following from Eq. (S4.66). Additionally, from Eq. (S4.61), it
we can show that ∆(z) = ∆0 +O  1z
follows that ImG0(z)< 0 (ImG0(z)> 0) for Imz > 0 (Imz < 0), meaning that G0(z) has no zeros above or below
the real axis. As a result, from its definition in Eq. (S5.46), it follows that ∆(z) has the same analytical properties as
G0 (z) – it is analytical below and above the real axis and also obeys ∆ (z∗) = ∆∗ (z), from Eq. (S4.66). As a result of
these properties and according to the lemma stated and proved in Appendix [V2f], ∆(z) is endowed with a spectral
representation akin to the one introduced in Eqs. (S5.49) and (S5.50).
  Tomoveforward, weapproximatetheintegralinEq.(S5.49)byaRiemannsum. Formally, asweshowinEq.(S5.52),
this is equivalent to approximating the spectral function ρ∆ (ω) by a series of Dirac δ-functions

                                                                NaX

whereVi andϵi are real numbers determined by fitting toρ∆ (ω). V 2i δ(ω−ϵi +µ) corresponds to a peak of density in
the spectral function ρ∆ (ω) at ω =ϵi−µ with weight V 2i . We reiterate that the purpose of introducing the Vi and
ϵi constants is to obtain the Hamiltonian reformulation of the single-site problem, within which certain exact results
can be more easily derived. Because the final results already summarized in Appendix [V2a] do not depend on Vi
and ϵi explicitly, we will leave them unspecified. Moreover, the number Na of δ-functions we use to approximate the
spectral function ρ∆ (ω) is also arbitrary – the larger Na is, the more accurate the approximation in Eq. (S5.51) is.

Using the Hamiltonian formulation from Eq. (S5.59), the exact properties of Σss (iωn) summarized in Appendix [V2a]
can be more easily derived, as we will show in Appendix [V2].
   The dynamics of thef-electrons within the single-site actionS ˆf,ˆass  (or, equivalently, the single-site HamiltonianHss)
is identical to the f-electron’s dynamics within the original single-site action without  ˆa electrons from Eq. (S4.43).
This is because by reversing the process in Eqs. (S5.56) to (S5.58) and integrating out the auxiliary electrons, one
can directly obtain Sss from S ˆf,ˆass . In other words, in the Hamiltonian formulation, the Na×Nf auxiliary fermions
have been introduced to “emulate” the effective Green’s function G0 (τ). Note also that the Na auxiliary fermions
introduced in the Hamiltonian formulation of the single-site problem render it impossible to solve exactly, due to the
prohibitively large Hilbert space dimension.

                                              d.  The ω→∞ behavior of Σss ω +i0+

  We are now in the position of proving the exact properties of the single-site dynamical self-energy summarized in
Appendix [V2a]. First, we consider the exact asymptotic form of the dynamical self-energy of the single-site model
at infinite frequency. To do so, we introduce the moments Mm of the interacting spectral function [272], which are
defined according to
                                                  Z ∞
                                          Mm =
                                                   −∞ dωωmρ (ω),  for  m∈ Z,m≥ 0.                         (S5.67)
The spectral function moments can be employed to construct an expansion around infinity of the interacting single-site
Green’s function using Eq. (S4.62) [272]. More precisely,
                                        Z ∞     dω            Z ∞    dω   ∞X   ω mρ (ω) =        ∞X   Mm
                              G (z) =         z−ωρ (ω) =              z         z                    zm+1.                (S5.68)
                                         −∞                     −∞       m=0                   m=0

Since the dynamical self-energy vanishes at complex infinity (i.e., limz→∞ Σss (z) = 0), as discussed in Eq. (S4.65),
one can also construct a similar expansion of Σss (z),

                                                                       ∞X  Cm

The large-frequency asymptotic form of Σss (ω +i0+) depends on the filling of the f-electrons nss, as well as on the
correlator between the filling of f-electrons belonging to different orbital, valley, and spin flavors,⟨nn⟩ss. In the next
Appendix [V2e], we will derive another way through which⟨nn⟩ss can be extracted from the interacting Green’s
function of the single-site model.
  Finally, we note that requiring that the IPT dynamical self-energy of the single-site model Σss (ω +i0+) obeys the
exact ω→∞ asymptotic behavior is equivalent to requiring that the first three moments of the interacting spectral
function of the single-site model are accurately captured within our IPT method.

                                                              f.  A useful lemma on spectral representation

                     We now state and prove a useful lemma used repeatedly throughout this section on the existence of a spectral
                  representation. Let f(z) be a complex function obeying the following properties:
                      1. f(z) is analytical above (Im(z)> 0) and below (Im(z)< 0) the real axis.
                      2. f (z∗) =f∗ (z).
                      3. f(z) tends to zero at least as fast as  1z for z→∞.
                  It follows that f(z) is endowed with a real spectral representation ρf (ω)
Im                                                          Z ∞

half plane (i.e.  Im (z′)> 0), we must have from Cauchy’s theorem that

                               3.    Details on the IPT method as applied to the THF model

   In Appendix [IV4], we outlined how the DMFT single-site problem corresponding to the THF model is solved using
the approximate IPT method [268–289]. By building on the exact properties of the single-site self-energy obtained
in Appendices [V1] and [V2], this section briefly reviews the IPT method in general and details its derivation in the
context of the THF model in the symmetric phase.
   The crux of the IPT approximation is to solve the single-site problem to second order in perturbation theory. By
definition, such an approximation is valid in the weakly interacting limit (i.e. the U1→ 0 limit), but is not generally
expected to hold in the strongly interacting or atomic limit (i.e.  in the U1 →∞ limit), which was discussed in
Appendix [V1]. However, as we will show in this section, both the second-order self-energy correction in the atomic
limit (which will be derived below) and the exact atomic-limit dynamical self-energy from Eq. (S5.22) have similar
functional forms. Based on this insight, the IPT method constructs an interpolated self-energy ansatz starting from
the second-order self-energy of the single-site model – which can be computed numerically from the effective single-site
Green’s functionG0 (iωn) according to Eq. (S4.70), as we will show explicitly below – and the atomic-limit self-energy
– which was derived analytically in Eq. (S5.22).
   The interpolated self-energy ansatz used in IPT depends on one constant and one function of ω, which are deter-
mined, respectively, by requiring that the interpolated self-energy has the exact ω→∞ asymptotic form dictated
by Eqs. (S5.42) and (S5.44)20 and that it recovers the exact atomic-limit self-energy in the U1→∞ limit. We will
then show that in the weakly interacting U1→ 0 limit this ansatz reduces to the second-order perturbative result
from Eq. (S4.70). By interpolating between two exact limits and having the correct ω→∞ behavior, we expect the
interpolated self-energy to correctly recover the intermediate interaction strength regime, as well.
   AsinAppendix[IV4], inthissection, wewillderiveaseriesofrelationsbetweenthedynamicalf-electronself-energy
and the effective Green’s function of the single-site model, G0 (iωn). Neither one of these two functions is known a
priori and thus need to be determined self-consistently. The algorithm that we use in our numerical implementation
was already detailed in Appendix [IV4b].





20 As mentioned in Appendix [V2a], this is equivalent to correctly reproducing the first three moments of the interacting spectral function
  of the single-site model.
                                                                                                                                             78

                                          a.  Second-order self-energy of the single-site model

  We start by considering the solution of the single-site model to second-order in perturbation theory. The calculation
of the corresponding second-order self-energy correction proceeds analogously to its computation in the symmetry-
broken THF phases from Appendix [III3]. There are only three differences:

   1.There is no  HU2 second-order self-energy contribution in the single-site model, since we focus on the correlation
       driven by the on-site interaction and this term is not included in Eq. (S4.43).

   2.Insteadofusingthefullydressed  f-electronpropagatorG (iωn)tocomputethesecond-ordercontribution, aswas
       done in Appendix [III3], one employs the Hartree-Fock propagator of the single-site model [269]. Anticipating
       our derivation of the IPT dynamical self-energy in Appendix [V3b], we will find it useful to work in terms of
       the family of propagators first defined in Eq. (S4.69) and repeated here for convenience

                                       G˜µ0 (iωn) =               1G−10   (iωn) + ˜µ−µ =               1iωn + ˜µ− ∆ (iωn),


       which is parameterized by the effective chemical potential ˜µ. G˜µ0 (iωn) can be thought of as the non-interacting
       Green’s function of the single-site model if the chemical potential of the latter was set to  ˜µ, as opposed to µ
       [while keeping the same effective hybridization function ∆ (iωn)]. The family of propagators G˜µ0 (iωn) includes
       both the non-interacting and the Hartree-Fock Green’s functions of the single-site model

                                  G˜µ0 (iωn)=G0 (iωn),   for    ˜µ =µ,                                        (S5.107)

                                  G˜µ0 (iωn)=GHF0   (iωn),  for    ˜µ =µ−U1           nss− 12     (Nf− 1).                 (S5.108)

       where the latter is the Hartree-Fock Green’s function of the single-site model

                                          GHF0   (iωn)≡                           1G−10   (iωn)−U1 nss−  1 (Nf− 1).                       (S5.109)
                                                                                      2
       When solving the single site model to second-order in perturbation theory, one should fix   ˜µ   = µ− (Nf− 1), thus ensuring that the Hartree-Fock single-site Green’s function is employed in com-
       U1 nss−  12

       setting U2→ 0) in Eqs. (S3.13) and (S3.14) to obtain
                                                      X        X

which are analogous to the ones defined in Eqs. (S3.23) and (S3.24) and in whichnF (ω) is the Fermi-Dirac distribution

already computed above the real axis, meaning that no analytical continuation is necessary.
  In principle, one can directly solve the single-site model to second-order in perturbation theory [by taking G˜µ0 (iωn)
to be the Hartree-Fock propagator of the single-site model GHF0   (iωn)], thereby approximating its exact dynamical
self-energy by the second-order contribution  Σss (iωn)≈  ˜Σf,(2) (iωn).  Within such an approach, the DMFT self-
consistency conditions are identical to ones depicted in Fig. 2. Due to its perturbative character, such a method is,
by definition, valid in the small-interaction regime.
  Thesecond-orderperturbativemethodforsolvingthesingle-sitemodelhasbeensuccessfullyappliedtothestandard
(i.e., spinful, single-band) Hubbard model in infinite dimensions at exactly half-filling [269, 316].  In this spinful,
single-band, particle-hole symmetric case,  ˜Σf,(2) (iωn) captures the correct behavior of the self-energy in both the
weakly and strongly interacting regimes [271, 294], accurately reproducing the associated Mott transition. However,
a naive extension of this approach away from particle-hole symmetry or beyond the single-band case yields unphysical
results in the strongly interacting limit [271].  We elaborate on these issues in Appendix [V3].  To address this
shortcoming, Refs. [268–289] have refined the original method of Ref. [269] by introducing an interpolated self-energy
constructed from the second-order perturbative result and the atomic self-energy solution in Eq. (S5.22).  In the
following Appendix [V3b], we discuss both the exact and second-order self-energies of the single-site model in the
atomic limit and present the construction and justification of the interpolated self-energy ansatz.


                                                     b.  The interpolated self-energy

  In Appendix [V3a], we outlined how the single-site model can be solved using perturbation theory in U1. While
this approach works well for the spinful single-band Hubbard model at the particle-hole symmetric filling n  =  12,
it fails to capture the correct physics in the strongly interacting regime for either n̸=  12 [271] or generically in the
multi-band case. In this section, we briefly justify this empirical observation and explain how IPT [268–289] addresses
this limitation.
  We begin with the expression for the second-order self-energy correction of the single-site model from Eq. (S4.70),
written in terms of the spectral function of the propagator G˜µ0 (iωn), and reproduced here for convenience
                                                       Z ∞        Z ∞        Z ∞

Eq. (S4.70) was derived from Eq. (S5.112) by performing the Matsubara summation as in Eq. (S3.19), followed by
analytic continuation. Our goal is to assess the applicability of the second-order perturbative solution in the U1→∞
limit (noting that in the U1→ 0 limit, the solution is valid by definition).
  Inthestronglyinteractingregime, thedetailedenergydependenceoftheeffectiveGreen’sfunctionG0 (iωn)becomes
unimportant. At energy scales on the order of U1, the non-interacting spectral function of the single-site model can
be approximated as a broadened Dirac δ-function (i.e., a Lorentzian). This behavior can be modeled by setting the
effective hybridization of the f-electrons, as defined in Eq. (S5.46), to ∆ (iωn) =iΓf, where Γf is a real broadening
parameter. With this choice, the effective spectral function from Eq. (S4.69) takes the Lorentzian form

   Comparing Eqs. (S5.120) and (S5.121), we find that near integer filling, the second-order perturbative result fails to

impurity problem perturbatively at n =  12 in the single-band case (Nf = 2) is remarkably successful for all U1: in the
U1→∞ limit, the exact and perturbative self-energies coincide [269, 271]. Furthermore, Eqs. (S5.120) and (S5.121)
show that away from particle-hole symmetry and in the strongly interacting regime, second-order perturbation theory
fails to reproduce the correct self-energy pole location, resulting in unphysical outcomes [271].
   Although the second-order perturbative expression does not correctly reproduce the pole locations and residues near
integer fillings, the exact and perturbative self-energies do share the same functional form. In particular, rewriting
Eq. (S5.118) in imaginary frequency

                                                          U21 (Nf− 1)n˜µ0      1−n˜µ0
                                                  iωn =          ˜Σf,(2),At (iωn)   − ˜µ,                            (S5.122)

one can express the exact atomic self-energy at integer f-electron filling νf =r− 4 (for 0<r< 8, r∈ Z) as
                                      ˜ΣAt (iωn)≈   1                     U21r (Nf−r)                      .                   (S5.123)
                                                      Nf  Nf  U21 (Nf−1)n˜µ0(1−n˜µ0)

Eq. (S5.123) has motivated Refs. [268, 270–281, 283, 284, 286, 287] to generalize this ansatz to arbitrary filling and
interaction strength.  The result is an interpolated self-energy, valid in both the weakly and strongly interacting
regimes, whose expression is given in Eq. (S4.71) and repeated here for convenience

                       ˜Σf,Int ω +i0+ =         a1
                                               ˜Σf,(2)(ω+i0+)−b (ω +i0+) =     a˜Σf,(2) (ω +i0+)1−b (ω +i0+) ˜Σf,(2) (ω +i0+).         (S5.124)

   In Eq. (S5.124), the constant a and the function b (ω +i0+) are determined by requiring that  ˜Σf,Int (ω +i0+) ex-
hibits the correct asymptotic behavior as ω→∞, as dictated by Eq. (S5.42), and that  ˜Σf,Int (ω +i0+) reproduces
the exact atomic-limit self-energy as U1→∞. Once a and b (ω +i0+) are fixed, we will also demonstrate in Ap-
pendix [V3f] that ˜Σf,Int (ω +i0+) correctly reproduces theU1→ 0 limit [273, 274, 278–281, 284, 286]. The self-energy
ansatz in Eq. (S5.124) is referred to as interpolated because  ˜Σf,Int (ω +i0+) reduces to the exact atomic limit for
U1→∞ and to the second-order perturbative result for U1→ 0. As an interpolation between two exact limits, it
is expected to provide a reliable approximation to the dynamical self-energy in the intermediate interaction strength
regime. Therefore, rather than using the second-order self-energy  ˜Σf,(2) (ω +i0+), we construct our self-consistent
solution based on the interpolated form  ˜Σf,Int (ω +i0+).
   Finally, following Refs. [268, 270–281, 283, 284, 286, 287], we compute the second-order self-energy correction
˜Σf,(2) (iωn) not using the propagator G0 (iωn) or GHF0   (iωn), but rather the newly introduced Green’s function
G˜µ0 (iωn). The fictitious chemical potential  ˜µ thus serves as an additional free (i.e. variational) parameter, which is
determined in simulations as described in Appendix [V3e].

we can use the asymptotic form of the second-order self-energy from Eq. (S5.117) to show that

                                             e.  Fixing the  ˜µ fictitious chemical potential

  As discussed at the end of Appendix [V3b], within the IPT method, the second-order self-energy correction
˜Σf,(2) (iωn), which forms the basis for the self-energy ansatz in Eq. (S5.124), is derived from G˜µ0 (iωn) – the ef-
fective Green’s function of the single-site model at chemical potential ˜µ. The interpolated self-energy thus depends on
                                                                                                                                             83

the fictitious chemical potential ˜µ and thef-electron fillingn˜µ0 of the non-interacting single-site model at ˜µ, in adition
to the actual f-electron fillingn and chemical potential µ. Theµ chemical potential is fixed at a given total filling of
the THF model, which, in turn, determinesn. Similarly,n˜µ0 is fully specified fromG˜µ0 (iωn) through Eq. (S4.73), once
a certain ˜µ chemical potential is assumed. The latter is thus a variational parameter of the problem: for fixedG0 (iωn)
and µ there exist an entire family of solutions parameterized by the fictitious chemical potential  ˜µ. A method for
fixing ˜µ in a way that renders the interpolated self-energy as close as possible to the exact result is thus necessary.
  Multiple methods have been proposed for fixing  ˜µ, with the most important ones being:

   1.Refs. [271–275, 277, 278, 280, 281, 284] fix    ˜µ by requiring that the interpolated self-energy ˜Σf,Int (ω +i0+) obeys
       Luttinger’s theorem [284]
                                           (Z ∞
                                   − 1π Im                               dω     G ω +i0+)             = 0.                  (S5.133)
                                               −∞ dωnF (ω) d˜Σf,Int (ω +i0+)

       Strictly speaking, Luttinger’s theorem is only valid at zero temperatures. As a result, previous studies either
       restrict to the zero temperature regime [271, 278, 281], impose Luttinger’s theorem at finite temperature [272–
       275, 277, 280], or use the  ˜µ chemical potential determined at zero temperature for finite temperature calcula-
       tions [284].

   2.Another method is to fix     ˜µ by requiring that n˜µ0  = n [268, 270, 272, 276, 277, 283]. It has been shown that
       imposingn˜µ0  =n approximately satisfies Luttinger’s theorem at zero temperature [270, 277], but has the added
       benefit of being usable at finite temperatures, as well. Moreover, this method has also been validated against
       exact diagonalization results [272].
   3.Finally, the simplest method is to take    ˜µ =µ. While this prescription does yield satisfactory results in the low
       interaction strength limit, it does not agree with exact diagonalization results for more general values of the
       interaction [272].
  In this work, we are interested exclusively in finite temperature results.  Owing to its proven reliability in this
context [272], we will employ the second method of fixing  ˜µ, which relies on imposing n =n˜µ0.


                                                     f.  Recovering the U1→ 0 limit

  So far we have verified that  ˜Σf,Int (ω +i0+) correctly recovers the large frequency behavior of the exact self-energy,
as well as the U1→∞ limit, wherein  ˜Σf,Int (ω +i0+) reduces to the dynamical self-energy of the atomic limit. One
still needs to show that  ˜Σf,Int (ω +i0+) correctly reproduces the U1→ 0 limit.
  As discussed in Appendix [V3e], in our implementation of IPT,  ˜µ is fixed such that n =n˜µ0. At the same time, in
the limit of vanishing interaction (U1→ 0),G (iωn)→G0 (iωn), which results in ˜µ→µ. In this limit, theb (ω +i0+)
function can be computed by noting that
                                                        h                                               i
                    ˜ΣAt ω +i0+ =       (Nf− 1)U21      nAt + (Nf− 2)⟨nn⟩At−n2At (Nf− 1)                           ,          (S5.134)
                                                                  ω +µ +i0+                                 +O U31
              ˜Σf,(2),At ω +i0+ = U21 (Nf− 1)n (1−n)ω +µ +i0+   ,                                              (S5.135)


                                 aAt = nAt + (Nf− 2)⟨nn⟩At− (Nf− 1)n2Atn (1−n)          ,                                (S5.136)

which follow, respectively, from Eqs. (S5.39), (S5.118) and (S5.130), by imposing n˜µ0→ n and  ˜µ→ µ. Substituting
Eqs. (S5.134) to (S5.136) in Eq. (S4.78), we obtain (for the U1 dependence)
                                                                                1
                                                         b ω +i0+ =O           U1    .                                 (S5.137)

Since  ˜Σf,(2) (ω +i0+) =O U21, we find from Eq. (S5.124) that

                                           ˜Σf,Int ω +i0+ =a˜Σf,(2) ω +i0+ +O U31               .                       (S5.138)
                                                                                                                                           84

We now note that in the vanishing interaction limit,⟨nn⟩ss =n2, and as a result, from Eq. (S5.126), we find that

                                     a = n + (Nf− 2)n2− (Nf− 1)n2                 =   n−n2        = 1,                    (S5.139)
                                                     n˜µ0 1−n˜µ0                   n˜µ0 1−n˜µ0

which then implies that indeed the exact low-U1 dynamical self-energy is recovered up to third-order corrections (i.e.,
˜Σf,Int (ω +i0+) is as accurate as  ˜Σf,(2) (ω +i0+) in the low interaction strength limit)
                                           ˜Σf,Int ω +i0+ = ˜Σf,(2) ω +i0+ +O U31.                        (S5.140)



                           Appendix VI: Additional result in many-body perturbation theory

  In this appendix, we derive two useful results for many-body perturbation theory. We start by introducing a general
fermionic Hamiltonian with quartic interaction. The interaction term is normal-ordered with respect to half-filling,
akin to the THF model interaction Hamiltonian from Appendix [I2]. We then express the internal energy of the
model in terms of its density matrix and self-energy correction. Finally, we compute the self-energy correction of this
model to second order in perturbation theory using the path integral formalism. Taking the interacting Hamiltonian
to be given by the HU1 and HU2 terms of the THF model from Eqs. (S1.12) and (S1.13), respectively, we re-derive
the second-order self-energy correction of the f-electrons, obtained already using a Feynman diagrammatic approach
in Appendix [III3].


                                                                1.    Model

  We will consider a general Hamiltonian of interacting fermions

                                                              H =H0 +HI,                                        (S6.1)

where

                     H0 =hijˆγ†i ˆγi                                                                      (S6.2)

                     HI = 12Vijkl     ˆγ†i ˆγj− 12δij    ˆγ†kˆγl− 12δkl  = 18Vijkl    ˆγ†i ˆγj− ˆγjˆγ†i ˆγ†kˆγl− ˆγlˆγ†k.          (S6.3)

In Eqs. (S6.2) and (S6.3) and throughout this appendix, we will employ Einstein’s summation convention, whereby
repeated indices are summed over. We considerN fermionic degrees of freedom denoted by ˆγ†i (where 1≤i≤N). The
hermitian Hamiltonian matrix h includes both the single-particle (i.e. kinetic) and chemical potential contributions,
given respectively by the two terms in

                                                             hij =h′ij−µδij.                                       (S6.4)

Additionally, V is a four-fermion interaction tensor. Expanding the interacting Hamiltonian as

                                   HI = 12Vijklˆγ†i ˆγ†kˆγlˆγj− 14Vkkijˆγ†i ˆγj− 14Vijkkˆγ†i ˆγj + 18Viijj,                    (S6.5)

we see that the four-fermion interaction tensor Vijkl can be chosen without loss of generality to obey Vijkl = Vklij,
such that an equivalent expression for HI is given by

                                            HI = 12Vijklˆγ†i ˆγ†kˆγlˆγj− 12Vijkkˆγ†i ˆγj + 18Viijj.                          (S6.6)

Finally, we note that because the chemical potential contribution is included implicitly inH0,H is the grand canonical
Hamiltonian of the system.
  The Green’s function of the system is defined as
                                                         D                E
                                                      −   Tτ ˆγi(τ)ˆγ†j (0) =Gij(τ),                                  (S6.7)
                                                                                                                                         85

where τ is the imaginary time,Tτ enforces the ordering of the operators that follow with respect to the imaginaryD ˆOE
time, and         denotes the expectation value of the operator  ˆO in the grand canonical ensemble
                                                                      h         i
                                                         D ˆOE     Tr  e−βH ˆO
                                                               =    Tr [e−βH] .                                     (S6.8)

The fermion operators are also evolved using the grand canonical ensemble HamiltonianH

                                                        ˆγ†i (τ) =eHτ ˆγ†i (0)e−Hτ.                                    (S6.9)

  The Fourier transformation of the Green’s function is given by
                                          Z β                                          X
                            Gij (iωn) =                                              β      Gij (iωn)e−iωnτ,               (S6.10)
                                            0   dτeiωnτGij(τ)  and Gij(τ) =  1         iωn

withβ = 1/T being the inverse temperature and ωn =  (2n+1)πβ   , the fermionic Matsubara frequencies. It is also useful
to define the non-interacting Matsubara Green’s function
                                                       D                E
                                                     −   Tτ ˆγi(τ)ˆγ†j (0)
                                                                          0 =G0ij(τ),                                 (S6.11)
whose definition is similar to Eq. (S6.7), with the only exception being that the imaginary time-evolution and the
averaging⟨...⟩0 in Eq. (S6.11) are performed within the non-interacting grand canonical ensemble Hamiltonian
H0. The non-interacting Green’s function can readily be expressed in terms of the single-particle Hamiltonian from
Eq. (S6.2),

                                                       G0 (iωn) = [iωn1−h]−1.                                  (S6.12)

  We also define the density matrix of the system
                   D      E                                                                    Gji(0+) +Gji(0−).         (S6.13)
            ϱij =   ˆγ†i ˆγj− 12δij =   limτ→0−Gji(τ)− 12δij =  limτ→0+Gji(τ) + 12δij = 12

In terms of the density matrix, the Hartree-Fock Hamiltonian matrix reads as

                                                      hMFij   =Vijklϱkl−Vilkjϱkl.                                  (S6.14)

where Σ (iωn) is the dynamical self-energy matrix of the system.


                                                         2.    Internal energy

  In this section, we will compute the internal energy of the system, which is defined as
                                                    D          E    D            E
                                              U =    H +µ ˆN      =   H0 +µ ˆN     +⟨HI⟩,                            (S6.16)

where  ˆN  = ˆγ†i ˆγi is the total number operator and we remind the reader that the internal energy is the expectation
value of the canonical, rather than the grand canonical Hamiltonian (i.e. without the chemical potential contribution).
The expectation value of the single-particle Hamiltonian can be readily expressed in terms of the density matrix of
the system
                                          D            E        D      E
                                           H0 +µ ˆN      =h′ij    ˆγ†i ˆγj=h′ij   ϱij + 12δij    .                         (S6.17)
                                                                                                                                          86

The expectation value of the interacting Hamiltonian requires a more careful treatment. We first note that at the
Hartree-Fock level,⟨HI⟩ can be readily computed using Wick’s theorem

                                              3.    Second-order self-energy correction

  In this section, we obtain the self-energy of the model from Eq. (S6.1) to second order in the interaction. To do so,
we first define the action of the system
                                                  Z β
                             Sˆγ†, ˆγ,η†,η =              ˆγ†i (τ)∂τ ˆγi(τ) +H + ˆγ†i (τ)ηi(τ) +η†i (τ)ˆγi(τ)
                                                    0   dτ
                                               =S0ˆγ†, ˆγ,η†,η +SIˆγ†, ˆγ,                                    (S6.29)
where the single-particle and interaction contributions are respectively given by
                       Z β      h                                                              i

                         0   dτ
                       Z β
        SIˆγ†, ˆγ =           8Vijkl    ˆγ†i (τ + 0+)ˆγj(τ) + ˆγ†i (τ)ˆγj(τ + 0+)      ˆγ†k(τ + 0+)ˆγl(τ) + ˆγ†k(τ)ˆγl(τ + 0+)      . (S6.31)
                         0   dτ 1
In Eq. (S6.29), we have introduced a source termηi(τ) for the ˆγ†i fermions. Note also that the interaction contribution
to the action was written using the second form of the interaction of HI from Eq. (S6.3). To control the relative
ordering of the fermionic operators appearing in the interaction, a small positive offset was added to the imaginary
time for the operator that is to be acted last.
  The partition function of the (interacting) system in the presence of a source reads as
                                                                Z
                                                  Zη†,η≡         Dˆγ, ˆγ†e−S[ˆγ†,ˆγ,η†,η].                             (S6.32)

     Gij (τ2−τ1) =             1         δ         δ                           =        δ         δ                               .    (S6.33)

  Our strategy for finding the second-order self-energy correction will be the following:
                                                                                                                                             89

   1.Starting from Dyson’s equation from Eq. (S6.15), we express the dynamical self-energy of the problem in powers
       of the interaction tensor and truncate it to second order

                                                    Σ (iωn) = Σ(2) (iωn) +O V 3,                               (S6.40)

       where Σ(2) (iωn) denotes the second-order contribution to the self-energy. Note that the static contribution to
       the self-energy hMF is first order in the interaction. Substituting Eq. (S6.40) into Eq. (S6.15), we find that

                       G (iωn) =G0 (iωn) +G0 (iωn)hMFG0 (iωn) +G0 (iωn)hMFG0 (iωn)hMFG0 (iωn)
                                 +G0 (iωn) Σ(2) (iωn)G0 (iωn) +O V 3,                                     (S6.41)

       which is just the expression of the full Green’s function of the system up to second order in the interaction.
   2.Using Eq. (S6.33), we compute the full Green’s function of the system perturbatively to second order in the
       interaction.
   3.By comparing the expression of the full Green’s function with the one in Eq. (S6.41), we can directly read off
       the first and second-order self-energy contributions.

A fully rigorous derivation also proves Dyson’s equation, which we assume to hold without proof.  The reader is
pointed to Ref. [317] for such a complete derivation. We now derive the interacting Green’s function of the system to
second order in the interaction.


                                              a.  Second-order interacting Green’s function

the interacting system in the presence of the source perturbatively in powers of the interaction tensor Vijkl. This can
be done by noting that from Eqs. (S6.29) and (S6.32)

We now expand the first exponential in powers of the interaction tensor. The functional derivatives with respect to
the source field will “pull down” Green’s functions from the exponential of the non-interacting partition function. The
functional derivatives with respect to the source fields contain small positive imaginary time offsets, which will result
in offsets in the argument of the non-interacting Green’s functionG0ij(τ). The latter is continuous for any value of
the imaginary time, except for τ  = 0, where it is discontinuous. As such, any small offset appearing in the Green’s
function can be safely ignored whenever the argument of the Green’s function is non-zero, but needs to be taken into
account when the Green’s function’s argument is vanishing.

symmetry of the interaction tensor Vijkl =Vklij assumed above Eq. (S6.6), we find that

                   Appendix VII: Numerical band structure results in the symmetry-broken states

  In this appendix, we present comprehensive numerical results on the band structures of the correlated ground state
candidates of TBG and TSTG from Table S1. The band structure calculations are performed using the second-order
self-consistent perturbation theory outlined in Appendix [III]. The results are summarized in Table S3 and then
presented in detail in the subsequent Appendices [VII2] to [VII10].
  For every positively filled correlated ground state candidate listed in Table S3, we determine the second-order self-
consistent solution for the integer-filled state. We also obtain the self-consistent solutions of the correlated phases
that result by doping the integer filled states, up to a maximum of ∆ν =±0.5. This methodology was explained in
Appendix [III4]. The band structures of each of the correlated ground state candidates are obtained at both low and
high temperatures and within TBG, TSTG with no displacement field (E = 0 meV), and TSTG with a small value of
the displacement field (E = 25 meV).
  For each graphene heterostructure we consider (TBG and TSTG), temperature, value of the displacement field
(in the case of TSTG), and correlated state, the results are shown in a single figure (e.g., Fig. S14).  To enable
easy comparisons between different band structures, we maintain consistent figure layouts. First, the total spectral
function, or the density of states (DOS)
                                                                  X    X
                                                   A (ω) =   1N0            Aiηs;iηs (ω, k),                               (S7.1)
                                                                   k   i,η,s
                                                                                                                                           95

is plotted in panel (a) of each figure as a function of ω and of the filling ν0− ∆ν≤ ν≤ ν0 + ∆ν (where ν0 is the
filling of the correlated state depicted in the figure and ∆ν = 0.5 is the maximal doping we consider). The k-resolved
spectral function

                                                     A (ω, k) =X       Aiηs;iηs (ω, k),                                 (S7.2)
                                                                  i,η,s

is then plotted in panels (b)-(d) of each figure along the high-symmetry lines of the moiré BZ for the integer-filled
correlated state at ν  = ν0 and for the correlated phases obtained by doping it to ν  = ν0± 0.2 (for ν0 ̸=  3), or
ν  = ν0± 0.1 (for ν0  = 3). We consider a small energy range around the Fermi level such that ω/meV∈ [−50, 50]
for TBG and ω/meV∈ [−75, 75] for TSTG. Both the k-resolved spectral function and the total density of states are
plotted using a color map where the saturation of the color is proportional to eitherA (ω), for panel (a), orA (ω, k),
for panel (b). The hue of the color map is related to the f-character % ˆf of the spectral function in that region, with
bluer regions corresponding to a larger f-character. The f-character is defined as

                           1 P    P                                                    P
                  % ˆf =  N0    k    α,η,sA(α+4)ηs;(α+4)ηs (ω, k)                         α,η,sA(α+4)ηs;(α+4)ηs (ω, k)
