for the DOS and k-resolved spectral function plots, respectively.
  Finally, we note that the results are shown only for the ν≥ 0 correlated ground state candidates from Table S1.
The results for the ν < 0 correlated phases can be inferred directly from the ν≥ 0 ones using the many-body charge
conjugation symmetries of TBG and TSTG discussed in Appendix [II3b].


                                                        1.    General discussion

  Our comprehensive numerical results of the correlated ground state candidates of the THF model reveal several
general features, which we summarize below, starting with TBG:
    • Near integer fillings  0≤ ν≤ 3, we observe that the gap between the flat bands above and below the Fermi
      energy near the KM point, when computed using second-order self-consistent perturbation theory, is reduced
      in comparison to the value obtained from Hartree-Fock calculations [186].  The gap at  KM acts as a proxy
      for the magnitude of the order parameter of the f-electrons [186]. Due to the more accurate representation of
      interaction-driven fluctuations in the second-order approach, there is a consequent decrease in the f-electron
      order parameter and in the associated gap at the KM point.

    • The shrinking of the gap in the second-order perturbative approach is more pronounced the further a state
      is from charge neutrality. For example, within Hartree-Fock theory, the|ν = 2, VP⟩ state shows an indirect
      gap [186]. However, such a gap is not present in second-order perturbative calculations. Considering the ν = 3
      states, self-consistent Hartree-Fock calculations indicate a gap of approximately  ∆ω ≈  30 meV at the  KM
      point [186]. This is in contrast to the second-order perturbation theory, which predicts a notably smaller gap of
      ∆ω≈ 15 meV. The reason for this difference lies in the behavior of thef-electrons for the 1≤ν≤ 3 integer-filled
      insulators. These electrons, which largely constitute the hole excitation bands, are nearer to the Fermi energy
      (or chemical potential) the more ν deviates from charge neutrality. This observation can be directly seen from
      the momentum-resolved spectral functions at integer filling 1≤ν≤ 3. Consequently, the f-electrons are more
      prone to charge fluctuations, which can potentially reduce the formation of f-electron local moments and thus
      reduce the order parameter formed by the f-electrons.

    • For 0≤ ν < 4 and close to integer fillings, the flat f-electron excitations at the moiré BZ boundary lead to
      sharp peaks in the DOS.

    • At a given filling 0≤ν < 4, the spectra of the valley- polarized and intervalley-coherent ground state candidates
      are broadly similar, as they are related by the approximate nonchiral flat U (4) symmetry [113, 186]. The latter
      is an exact symmetry of the system in the flat M  =  0 limit. One notable exception already revealed both
      numerically and analytically within Hartree-Fock theory [186], for  0≤ ν≤ 2, the intervalley-coherent states
      have a larger gap at the  ΓM point. This larger gap is responsible for their energy being lower than that of
      valley-polarized states in the absence of strain.

    • The spectrum computed around ν = 0 is particle hole-symmetric, due to theP symmetry of TBG. For small
      doping around the charge neutrality point, both the electron and the hole excitations consist of light fermions
                                                                                                                                        96

  with predominantly c-character. Being far from the Fermi energy (chemical potential), the f-electrons do not
  experience interaction-driven fluctuations at small levels of doping. In effect, the doped band structure is almost
  identical to the undoped one, up to a shift in energy. This is confirmed in the DOS plots, which reveal a two-peak
  structure stemming from the f-electron flat bands near the moiré BZ boundary: for doping up to|∆ν| ≲ 0.1
  at low temperatures or|∆ν| ≲ 0.05 at high temperatures, the two f-electron peaks move rigidly with doping.
  As doping is increased, the f-electron excitations are brought closer to the Fermi energy and thus experience
  larger interaction-driven fluctuations. This results in a shrinking of the gap at KM between the f-electron flat
  bands and a more incoherent peak of the f-electron bands in the k-resolved spectral function and the DOS.
  This effect is more pronounced at higher temperatures. The resulting DOS plots are similar in shape to the
  local DOS measured by scanning tunneling microscopy experiments in the ultra-low strain device of Ref. [38].

• Nearν = 1 orν = 2, when doping is small, the electron excitations consist of light fermions with predominantly
  c-character near the  ΓM point.  In contrast, the hole excitations are mostly given by heavy electrons with
  predominantlyf-character at the edge of the moiré BZ. We also point out that the light-fermion bands at ν = 2
  cross the Fermi energy and create a small Fermi surface near ΓM point, which is different from the Hartree-Fock
  result [186].

• Due to the markedly different nature of the electron and hole charge-one excitations around the  1≤ ν≤  2
  insulators, the electron and hole doped regions are very different:

     – The electron doping case is qualitatively similar to the charge neutrality case: for small electron doping,
        the electrons near the Fermi energy have c-character and experience little interaction-driven fluctuations,
        leaving the f-electron order parameter relatively unaffected. In this case, the doped bands are simply an
        energy-shifted version of the undoped ones. The gap at  KM between the f-electron flat bands remains
        relatively constant and the DOS plots show a two-peak structure with relatively sharp f-electron peaks
        which move parallel to one another as a function of doping.
     – Astheelectrondopingisfurtherincreased,f-electronsarebroughtclosetotheFermilevel. Asaresult, they
        experience larger interaction-driven fluctuations which diminish their order parameter leading to increased
        incoherence of their spectral signal and a shrinking of the gap at KM.
     – In the hole doping case, the f-electrons from the hole band are brought close to the Fermi energy, which
        leads to large charge fluctuations of the f-electrons. Their spectral signal becomes incoherent and the gap
        at the KM point shrinks with doping. In the DOS plots, the hole f-electron peak remains pinned close to
        zero energy, while the electron one moves towards zero energy as hole doping is increased.
     – The effects of interaction-driven fluctuations increases with increasing temperatures.

• For the doped ν  =  1 correlated insulators, at low temperature, a three-peaked structure can be seen in the
  DOS plots forν≈ 0.6. This is due to a phase transition where the f-electron order parameter changes between
  different orders (one more f-electron per unit cell becomes filled).

• For the ν = 3 correlated state, we find that:

     – The system does not have an indirect gap, with c-electrons already forming gapless charge-one excitations
        around the Fermi energy, which is also similar to ν  =  2.  As a result, for very small electron or hole
        doping|∆ν| ≲ 0.1, the main fermionic species around the Fermi energy are still c-electrons and the DOS
        dependence with ν is consistent with a rigid doping behavior.
     – For larger doping|∆ν| ≳ 0.1, the f-electron bands are brought closer to the Fermi energy. As a result,
        large interaction-driven fluctuations diminish the order parameter of the system, the gap at KM decreases,
        and the two f-electron peaks in the DOS merge together. At around|∆ν|∼ 0.3, the system undergoes an
        order-disorder transition and the order is lost.

• As already pointed out in Table S3, the high-temperature chosen for the 0≤ν≤ 2 correlated insulator is close
  to the critical temperature at which the system start transitioning to the disordered (symmetric) phase within
  the doping range we consider. The critical temperatures obtained through second-order perturbation are close
  to the DMFT results [4]. This is in stark contrast with the finite-temperature Hartree-Fock calculations, which
  overestimate the critical temperature by an order of magnitude [4].

• The band structure calculations at low and high temperatures are qualitatively similar, besides the fact that
  the f-electron excitations become more incoherent at higher temperatures.
                                                                                                                                              97

    • The band structure of the ν  =  4 band insulator is largely unaffected by the addition of the second-order
       perturbative correction. The latter only leads to more incoherent f-electron excitations.
  The spectral functions of TSTG in the symmetry-broken case, whether a displacement field is present or not, closely
resemble those of TBG. The primary difference is the inclusion of the highly dispersive d-electron bands:
    • The Dirac cone formed by thed-electrons is not pinned at charge neutrality and, therefore, slightly changes the
       relative filling of the f- and c-electrons away from charge neutrality.
    • For ν > 0, the filling of the d-electrons νd is slightly larger than zero (with νd increasing with ν). Relative to
       the TBG case at the same overall filling, the TBG bands within TSTG are slightly hole doped.
    • In the presence of displacement field, the d- and f-electrons hybridize slightly near the KM point.

                       Appendix VIII: Numerical band structure results in the symmetric state

  This appendix presents comprehensive numerical results for the interacting spectral functions of TBG and TSTG
in the symmetric phase.  The calculations are carried out using the IPT method described in Appendix [IV] and
elaborated in Appendix [V]. A summary of the results is provided in Table S4, with a detailed discussion following in
Appendix [VIII3].
  We begin by briefly highlighting the key features of the THF spectral function in the symmetric phase. We then
introduce the Hubbard-I approximation [290, 291] to describe the dynamical self-energy of the THF model in the
symmetric state [199–201]. This allows us to analytically interpret the numerically obtained spectral functions near
integer fillings, particularly the emergence of gap-opening-like features.
  For each combination of moiré system and parameter set listed in Table S4, we compute the interacting spectral
function in the symmetric state using the IPT method. The corresponding results are displayed in a single figure for
each case (e.g., Fig. S69). To facilitate direct comparison between different band structures, we adopt a consistent
figure layout throughout.
  In each figure, panel (a) shows the total spectral functionA(ω) – as defined in Eq. (S7.1) – plotted as a function
of ω and the filling ν in the range  0≤ ν≤  4.5. Panels (b)-(j) display the k-resolved spectral function along the
high-symmetry lines of the moiré Brillouin zone at positive integer and half-integer fillings, as indicated in each panel.
Both the k-resolved spectral function and the total density of states are rendered using the same colormap as in
Appendix [VII].
  Consistent with Appendix [VII], we show results only for ν≥ 0. The corresponding ν < 0 results are obtained by
applying the many-body charge conjugation symmetries of TBG and TSTG, as discussed in Appendix [II3b].


                                                        1.    General discussion

  We now discuss the key features of the spectral functions obtained in the symmetric-state calculations:

    • We begin with the high-temperature (T  = 50 K) TBG spectral function shown in Fig. S70. At elevated tem-
       peratures, the dominant features are the Hubbard bands formed by the f-electrons. As the filling is varied,
       the energy of these bands exhibits a cascade of transitions. These transitions originate from changes between
       distinct f-electron occupancy states and can be understood in the zero-hybridization limit of the model [251].

    • As the temperature is lowered toT  = 20 K, hybridization between thef- andc-electrons leads to the emergence
       of a Kondo resonance (i.e., a zero-energy spectral peak) at specific fillings – such as ν = 1.5,ν = 2.5, andν ≳ 3
       – as shown in Fig. S69. In contrast, no Kondo resonance is observed near the integer fillings ν = 0 and ν = 1.

    • As the temperature is further reduced to T  = 5 K, Fig. S14 shows that a Kondo resonance begins to develop at
       nearly all fillings. The corresponding zero-energy peak becomes noticeably sharper than at T  = 20 K.

    • The spectral function of TSTG exhibits behavior similar to that of TBG. At high temperatures (Figs. S18
       and S19), we observe Hubbard bands and a cascade of transitions. Upon lowering the temperature, a Kondo
       resonance emerges, producing a sharp zero-energy peak at very low temperatures (Figs. S15 and S16).
                                                                                                                                          117

    • The coexistence of these two features – the Hubbard bands and the Kondo peak – gives the spectral function
      a characteristic “tail-feather” appearance, as seen for example in Fig. S14. This structure is consistent with
      observations from various spectroscopy experiments [22, 25, 38, 64, 65, 74].

  A comparison between the DMFT results obtained using the IPT impurity solver and those based on a QMC
impurity solver [2, 4] reveals excellent qualitative agreement, although some quantitative differences remain:
    • DMFT simulations with a QMC impurity solver suggest that Kondo physics is irrelevant at the integer fillings
      ν = 0, 1, 2, down to temperatures as low asT∼ 2 K [2]. While our IPT-based approach also shows a suppressed
      Kondoeffectatintegerfillingscomparedtonon-integerones, itdoespredictaslightlyhigherKondotemperature:
      a small Kondo peak is still discernible around integer fillings even at T  = 5 K. At non-integer fillings, both our
      results and those from QMC-based DMFT consistently indicate a strong tendency toward Kondo physics [2].
    • Since the IPT method only approximately satisfies the Luttinger theorem (as discussed in Appendix [V3e]),
      the gap-opening-like features observed in the QMC-DMFT solution [4], which occur almost exactly at integer
      fillings, appear slightly displaced in our IPT simulations – by approximately 0.1 in filling. This shift is visible
      in panel (a) of the figures in Appendix [VIII3].
  In DMFT simulations employing either the IPT or QMC impurity solver, we find that the symmetric solution
exhibits gap-opening behavior – or, at the very least, a significant suppression of the numerically computed density of
states at the Fermi level – around non-zero integer fillings. To provide analytical insight into this feature, we introduce
the Hubbard-I approximation for the dynamical self-energy of the symmetric phase in the following Appendix [VIII2],
and analyze the spectrum of TBG within the THF model at integer fillings.


                                                   2.    Hubbard-I approximation

  In this section, we describe the Hubbard-I approximation, as applied to the THF model. Within this approach,
the interacting Green’s function of a system is obtained using the atomic self-energy.  While this approximation
captures the formation of Hubbard bands, it does not account for the emergence of the Kondo resonance. However,
as demonstrated in Ref. [2], the Kondo effect becomes less relevant near integer fillings. This makes the Hubbard-I
approximation particularly suitable for explaining the behavior of electrons in this regime.
  Here, weusetheHubbard-Iapproximationtoanalyzethegap-openingbehaviorobservedinthesymmetricstatenear
integer fillings. In Appendix [VIII2b], we present the Hubbard-I approximation in detail and derive the corresponding
Green’s function. We also show how this approach captures the emergence of gaps in the interacting spectrum at
non-zero integer fillings.


                        a.  Mapping an interacting Green’s function to a non-interacting Green’s function

  Before presenting the results of the Hubbard-I approximation applied to the THF model, we first outline a general
method for expressing an interacting Green’s function in terms of an equivalent non-interacting Green’s function by
introducing auxiliary fermionic degrees of freedom, as also discussed in Ref. [199].
  To illustrate this, we consider a generic multi-orbital system with electron operators  ˆγ†i, where the index i may
represent spin, momentum, orbital, or sublattice labels. The system is described by the following generic interacting
Hamiltonian
                                                ˆH =X     tijˆγ†i ˆγj + X   Uijlmˆγ†i ˆγjˆγ†l ˆγm                             (S8.1)

                                                      i,j            i,j,l,m

where tij denotes the hopping matrix elements, and Uijlm encodes the interaction terms.
  The interacting Green’s function of the system is defined as
                                              D                E
                                 Gij(τ) =−      Tτ ˆγi(τ)ˆγ†j (0) ,
                                            Z β                      h iωn1−t−hMF− Σ (iωn)−1i

                                             0   dτeiωnτGij(τ) =                                             ij,                (S8.2)

where hMFij  and Σij (iωn) represent the static and dynamical self-energy contributions, respectively. The static part
corresponds to the Hartree-Fock mean-field interaction Hamiltonian.

  The dynamical self-energy itself admits a spectral representation [298, 299]

                                          ρΣ(ω) =−  1       Σ ω +i0+− Σ† ω +i0+                          (S8.4)

  Similar to the spectral function of the Green’s function defined in Eq. (S8.2), ρΣ(ω) is a Hermitian, positive semi-
definite matrix. Hermiticity,

                                                         ρΣ(ω) = ρΣ(ω)†,                                      (S8.5)

follows directly from Eq. (S8.4). To see that ρΣ(ω) is also positive semi-definite, we note that from Eq. (S8.2), the

                                      Σ ω +i0+ =ω +i0+−t−hMF−G−1 ω +i0+.                       (S8.6)

                       ρΣ(ω) =   12πi   G−1 ω +i0+−G† ω +i0+−1

which is manifestly a positive semi-definite matrix.
  The fact that ρΣ(ω) is Hermitian and positive semi-definite allows it to be expressed as a sum of δ-functions
multiplied by positive semi-definite matrices.  These matrices can, without loss of generality, be written as outer
products of vectors
                                                  ρΣij(ω)≈X      vαi vαj∗δ(ω−ϵα)                                (S8.8)

                                                              α
where vαi are complex vectors and ϵα are real frequencies. The parameters vαi and ϵα are introduced to accurately
describe the spectral function ρΣ. An arbitrarily good approximation can be achieved by including sufficiently many
terms in the sum in Eq. (S8.8). In the following, we assume that a sufficient number of terms have been included so
that the approximation is effectively exact. Using the spectral representation of the self-energy from Eq. (S8.3), we
then obtain                                                                 vαj ∗

 ˆγ†

                            A B −1                                                                        !

                                                        D                 Eaux,

                                                         Gaux,γ (iωn) =G (iωn).                                   (S8.15)

  We have thus shown that the interacting Green’s function of the original interacting Hamiltonian H can be equiv-
alently described by the Green’s function of an appropriately constructed non-interacting Hamiltonian Haux. While
this mapping does not provide a method for computing the interacting Green’s function directly, it enables a more
convenient framework for analyzing the band topology of the interacting system through the lens of an effective
non-interacting model.


                                 b.  The THF Green’s function within the Hubbard-I approximation

  We now introduce the Green’s function of the THF model within the Hubbard-I approximation. At integer fillings
and in the low-temperature limit (βU1≫ 1), the self-energy of the f-electrons in the atomic limit was derived in
Eq. (S5.37), and is reproduced here for convenience

                      ˜ΣAt (iωn)≈   1         U21r (Nf−r)
                                     N2f  iωn−U1(r/Nf−  12),  for  n = rNf   with  r∈ Z, 0≤r≤Nf,          (S8.16)

where r denotes the number of filled f-electron flavors (we assume an integer number of f-electron bands are filled,
as in the zero-hybridization model of Ref. [251]). In the Hubbard-I approximation, the interacting Green’s function
is computed by approximating the dynamical self-energy of the system with its atomic-limit expression

                                  GHubbard-I (iωn, k) =(iωn +µ) 1−hMF (k)− ΣAt (iωn)−1,                  (S8.17)

                         ϵc,1 =−µ + V (0)Ω0 νc +W1νf,                                                    (S8.20)

  In Eq. (S8.19), νc,a denotes the filling of c electrons with orbital index a, whileϵc,1,ϵc,2, andϵf denote the relative
energies of the c- and f-electrons and include the chemical potential and the Hartree contributions stemming from
the HV, HW, and HU1 terms of the THF interaction Hamiltonian. The corresponding Fock contributions – which
are ignored in Eq. (S8.19) – could, in principle, further renormalize the f–c hybridization. However, since we are
interested in the overall features of the spectrum of TBG in the symmetric state (such as gap openings), these effects
are less important and have been ignored here, as have the other terms of the interacting Hamiltonian, namely HJ,
H ˜J, HK, and HU2.  In what follows, we will restrict ourselves to integer total fillings ν ∈  Z (with|ν|≤  3) and
approximate the filling of thef- andc-electrons by their zero-hybridization solution [251], wherebyνc = 0 andνf =ν.
  We aim to describe the spectrum of the THF model of TBG under the Hubbard-I approximation by studying the
Green’s function from Eq. (S8.17). We first employ the idea introduced in Ref. [199] and map the interacting THF
Hamiltonian within the Hubbard-I approximation to an effective non-interacting one. To do so, we first note that the
atomic self-energy from Eq. (S8.16) admits the following spectral representation
                           Z ∞    ρ˜ΣAt(ω)

         ˆHaux = X       hMFiηs;i′η′s′ (k) ˆγ†k,i,η,sˆγk,i′,η′,s′

is diagonal in the f-electron fermion flavors. Within the Hubbard-I approximation, the self-energy for each flavor
contains a single pole. Consequently, we introduce one auxiliary fermion per flavor, coupled to the corresponding
f-electron flavor. The Hamiltonian in Eq. (S8.24) yields the same Green’s function (for the f- and c-electrons) and
the same single-particle spectrum as in Eq. (S8.17).

We observe that, for each valley and spin, the c-electrons develop a node between Ec,2(k) and Ec,3(k) at the  ΓM
point, where Ec,2(k = 0) =Ec,3(k = 0).
  We now analyze the effect of hybridization and focus on the evolution of the nodes formed by the c-electrons.
We first note that, due to the formation of Hubbard bands, in the zero-hybridization limit the f- and a-electrons
are gapped. As such, we can treat them as high-energy degrees of freedom that hybridize with the low-energy c-
electrons. This allows us to treat the hybridization terms γ and v′⋆ perturbatively. To proceed, we first rewrite the
f-c hybridization term in the g-electron basis, which gives

In the limit v′⋆ = 0, the resulting dispersion simplifies to
                                  s M−m 2                                                       s M−m 2
    Ec,1 (k) =ϵc + M +m2  −                2        +v2⋆|k|2,  Ec,2 (k) =ϵc + M +m2      +               2        +v2⋆|k|2,
                                  s M +m 2                                                      s M +m 2

with

                                                     m =−γ2  u2Ef,1 + v2Ef,2       .                                 (S8.35)

  Therefore, for each valley and spin, there are four bands near the Fermi energy and close to the ΓM point. We now
discuss whether the system is gapped at integer fillings. In practice, near integer filling, for each valley and each spin,
the lowest two (c-electron) bands described by Eq. (S8.34) will be filled. Exactly at the ΓM point, we find

                         Ec,1 (0) =ϵc + m +M−|M−m|2      ,  Ec,2 (0) =ϵc + m +M +|M−m|2      ,

                         Ec,3 (0) =ϵc + m−M−|M +m|2      ,  Ec,4 (0) =ϵc + m−M +|M +m|2      .             (S8.36)

  Depending on the value of M, three distinct scenarios can occur:
    • Case I:|m|<M:

                                              Ec,1 (0) =ϵc +m,  Ec,2 (0) =ϵc +M
                                              Ec,3 (0) =ϵc−M,  Ec,4 (0) =ϵc +m                            (S8.37)

      In this case, there is a node formed by the middle two bands Ec,1(k) andEc,4(k), and the system (with the two
      lowest bands fully filled) is gapless.
    • Case II: m>M:

                                             Ec,1 (0) =ϵc +M,  Ec,2 (0) =ϵc +m,
                                             Ec,3 (0) =ϵc−M,  Ec,4 (0) =ϵc +m,                            (S8.38)

      Here, the upper two bandsEc,2(k) andEc,4(k) form a node. In this case, the system (with the two lowest bands
      filled) acquires a gap near the ΓM point.
    • Case III: m<−M:

                                             Ec,1 (0) =ϵc +m,  Ec,2 (0) =ϵc +M,
                                             Ec,3 (0) =ϵc +m,  Ec,4 (0) =ϵc−M,                            (S8.39)

      In this situation, a node forms between the lower two bands Ec,1(k) and Ec,3(k). As in the previous case, the
      system (with its two lowest bands filled) becomes gapped near the ΓM point.

  It is worth noting that at charge neutrality, particle-hole symmetry enforces m = 0, and the system is gapless with
nodes. At non-zero integer fillings, we are generally in the regime|M|<|m|, and the system develops a finite gap.
